# Rio de Janeiro

This article is about the city. For the state, see [Rio de Janeiro (state)](229419.md). For other uses, see [Rio de Janeiro (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_\(disambiguation\)).

**Rio de Janeiro**,[^cite_note-9] also known simply as **Rio**,[^cite_note-10] is the capital and largest city of the [state of Rio de Janeiro](229419.md). It is the [second-largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Brazil_by_population) in Brazil after [São Paulo](390875.md), and the largest by international tourists. It has a population of 6 million in the city proper, and 13 million people in its metropolitan area as of 2025. It is also the [sixth-most-populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_cities_in_the_Americas) in the [Americas](29833.md).

Founded in 1565, the city was initially the seat of the [Captaincy of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_of_Rio_de_Janeiro), a domain of the [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire). In 1763, it became the capital of the [State of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Brazil). In 1808, when the [Portuguese Royal Court moved to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_Court_to_Brazil), Rio de Janeiro became the seat of the court of Queen [Maria I of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_I_of_Portugal). Under the leadership of her son, Prince Regent [John of Braganza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_VI_of_Portugal), Maria raised Brazil to the dignity of a kingdom within the [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil, and Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves). Rio remained as the capital of the [pluricontinental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluricontinentalism) monarchy until 1822, when the [Brazilian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_War_of_Independence) began. This is the only instance in modern history that the capital of a colonizing country officially shifted to a city in one of its colonies. Rio de Janeiro subsequently served as the capital of the [Empire of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil) until 1889, and then the capital of republican Brazil until 1960, when the capital was moved to [Brasília](4752.md).

Rio de Janeiro has the second largest municipal [GDP](12594.md) in the country,[^cite_note-IBGE_PIB_Ranking-11] and 30th-largest in the world as of 2008,[^cite_note-Richest_Cities-12] estimated at [R\$](34201323.md)343 billion. It is home to the headquarters of major Brazilian oil, mining, and telecommunications companies, including two of the country's largest corporations, [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras) and [Vale](1873300.md), and Latin America's largest telemedia conglomerate, [Grupo Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_Globo). The home of many universities and institutes, it is the second-largest center of research and development in Brazil, accounting for 17 percent of national scientific output according to 2005 data.[^cite_note-Unicamp_2005-13] Despite the perception of a high crime rate, the city actually has a lower [incidence of crime](15348345.md) than most state capitals in Brazil.[^cite_note-14]

Rio de Janeiro is one of the most visited cities in the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere) and is known for its natural settings, [carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Carnival), [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba), [bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova), and beaches such as [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca), [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema), and [Leblon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leblon).[^cite_note-15] In addition to the beaches, other landmarks include the statue of [*Christ the Redeemer*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_\(statue\)) atop [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado) mountain, named one of the [New Seven Wonders of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New7Wonders_of_the_World); [Sugarloaf Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain) with its [cable car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Cable_Car); the *[Sambódromo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambadrome_Marqu%C3%AAs_de_Sapuca%C3%AD)*, a permanent grandstand-lined parade avenue which is used during Carnival; and [Maracanã Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracan%C3%A3_Stadium), one of the [world's largest football stadiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_association_football_stadiums_by_capacity). Rio de Janeiro was the host of the [2016 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics) and [Paralympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Paralympics), making the city the first South American and [Portuguese-speaking city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_where_Portuguese_is_an_official_language) to host these events, and the third time that the [Olympics](22576.md) were held in a Southern Hemisphere city.[^cite_note-16] The Maracanã Stadium held the finals of the [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup) and [2014](656933.md) FIFA World Cups and the [2013 FIFA Confederations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_FIFA_Confederations_Cup). The city also hosted the [XV Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pan_American_Games) in 2007, and the [G20 summit in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_G20_Rio_de_Janeiro_summit), and will host the [FIFA Women's World Cup in 2027](60983910.md).[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18]

## History

Main articles: [History of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) and [Timeline of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Rio_de_Janeiro)

Historical affiliations

> [Portugal](23033.md) [Portuguese Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) 1565–1815\
>  [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves) 1815–1822\
>  [Empire of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil) 1822–1889\
> [Brazil](3383.md) [Republic of Brazil](3383.md) 1889–present

### Pre-Cabraline period

Main article: [Pre-Cabraline history of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Cabraline_history_of_Brazil)

The region of Rio was inhabited by the [Tupi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupi_people), [Puri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pur%C3%AD_people), [Botocudo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botocudo_people) and [Maxakalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxakal%C3%AD_language) peoples.[^cite_note-19]

### Colonial period



Founding of Rio de Janeiro on 1 March 1565. The painting depicts the Governor General of Brazil, [Mem de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mem_de_S%C3%A1), presenting the [key to the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Key_to_the_city) to the *alcaide*.

Europeans first encountered [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay) on 1 January 1502 (hence Rio de Janeiro, "January River"), during a [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people) expedition under explorer [Gaspar de Lemos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaspar_de_Lemos), captain of a ship in [Pedro Álvares Cabral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_%C3%81lvares_Cabral)'s fleet, or under [Gonçalo Coelho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gon%C3%A7alo_Coelho).[^cite_note-20] Allegedly the Florentine explorer [Amerigo Vespucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amerigo_Vespucci) participated as an observer at the invitation of [King Manuel I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_I_of_Portugal) in the same expedition.

In 1555, one of the islands of Guanabara Bay, now called [Villegagnon Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villegagnon_Island), was occupied by 500 French colonists under the French [admiral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admiral) [Nicolas Durand de Villegaignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Durand_de_Villegaignon). Consequently, Villegagnon built [Fort Coligny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Coligny) on the island when attempting to establish the [France Antarctique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France_Antarctique) colony. Eventually, this French settlement became too much of a threat to the established Portuguese colony, and in 1560 the order was made to get rid of them. A years-long military aggression was then initiated by the new Governor General of Brazil [Mem de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mem_de_S%C3%A1), and later continued by his nephew [Estácio de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Est%C3%A1cio_de_S%C3%A1). On 20 January 1567, a final defeat was imposed on the French forces, and they were decisively expelled from Brazil for good.



Between 500 thousand and one million Africans were trafficked at [Valongo Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valongo_Wharf) through the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md)

[^cite_note-21]

The city of Rio de Janeiro proper was founded on 1 March 1565 by the Portuguese, led by Estácio de Sá, including [Antônio de Mariz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B4nio_de_Mariz?action=edit\&redlink=1). It was named *São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro*, in honor of St. Sebastian, the [saint who was the namesake and patron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint's_name) of the Portuguese then-monarch [Sebastião](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_of_Portugal). *Rio de Janeiro* was the name of Guanabara Bay. Until early in the 18th century, the city was threatened or invaded by several mostly French pirates and buccaneers, such as [Jean-François Duclerc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Duclerc) and [René Duguay-Trouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Duguay-Trouin).[^cite_note-22]

In the late 17th century, [Bandeirantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandeirantes) discovered gold and diamonds in the neighboring [captaincy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy) of [Minas Gerais](222651.md), thus Rio de Janeiro became a much more practical port for exporting diversified sources of wealth (gold, precious stones, besides sugar) than [Salvador, Bahia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador,_Bahia), much farther northeast. On 27 January 1763,[^cite_note-RobinsonRobinson2014-23] the colonial administration in Portuguese America was moved from Salvador to Rio de Janeiro. The city remained primarily a colonial capital until 1808, when the Portuguese royal family and most of the associated [Lisbon](18091.md) nobles, fleeing from [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_I)'s invasion of Portugal, [moved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_Court_to_Brazil) to Rio de Janeiro.

### Portuguese royal period

Main article: [Transfer of the Portuguese court to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_the_Portuguese_court_to_Brazil)



*View of the bay and the entry of the city of Rio as seen from the terrace of the convent of Santo Antônio*, by [Nicolas-Antoine Taunay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas-Antoine_Taunay) (1816)

The kingdom's capital was transferred to the city, which, thus, became the only European capital outside of [Europe](9239.md). As there was no physical space or urban structure to accommodate hundreds of noblemen who arrived suddenly, many inhabitants were evicted from their homes.[^cite_note-24] In the first decade, several educational establishments were created, such as the Military Academy, the Royal School of Sciences, Arts and Crafts and the [Imperial Academy of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Imperial_de_Belas_Artes), as well as the [National Library of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil) – with the largest collection in [Latin America](18524.md)[^cite_note-BNB-25] – and The [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Botanical_Garden). The first printed newspaper in Brazil, the *[Gazeta do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazeta_do_Rio_de_Janeiro)*, came into circulation during this period.[^cite_note-26] When Brazil was elevated to Kingdom in 1815, it became the capital of the [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves) until the return of the [Portuguese Royal Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Royal_Family) to [Lisbon](18091.md) in 1821, but remained as capital of the [Kingdom of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Brazil).[^cite_note-HCRJ-27]

From the colonial period until the first independent era, Rio de Janeiro was a city of enslaved people. There was a large influx of [African slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Brazil) to Rio de Janeiro: in 1819, there were 145,000 slaves in the captaincy. In 1840, the number of enslaved people reached 220,000.[^cite_note-28] Between 1811 and 1831, 500,000 to a million enslaved people arrived in Rio de Janeiro through [Valongo Wharf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valongo_Wharf), which is now a [World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-29] The Port of Rio de Janeiro was the largest enslaved people port in America.[^cite_note-30]

### Imperial period



Botafogo Bay in 1869



Botafogo Bay in 1889

When [Prince Pedro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_I_of_Brazil) proclaimed the [independence of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Declaration_of_Independence) in 1822, he decided to keep Rio de Janeiro as the capital of his [new empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil) while the place was enriched with sugar cane agriculture in the Campos region and, especially, with the new coffee cultivation in the [Paraíba Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C3%ADba_Valley).[^cite_note-HCRJ-27] In order to separate the province from the capital of the Empire, the city was converted in Neutral Municipality in 1834, passing the [province of Rio de Janeiro](229419.md) to have [Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niter%C3%B3i) as capital.[^cite_note-HCRJ-27]

As a political center of the country, Rio concentrated the political-partisan life of the Empire. It was the main stage of the abolitionist and republican movements in the last half of the 19th century.[^cite_note-HCRJ-27] At that time, the number of enslaved people was drastically reduced, and the city was developed, with modern drains, animal trams, train stations crossing the city, gas and electric lighting, telephone and telegraph wiring, water and river plumbing.[^cite_note-HCRJ-27] Rio continued as the capital of Brazil after 1889, when the monarchy was [replaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_Republic_\(Brazil\)) by a republic.

On 6 February 1889, the Bangu Textile Factory was founded, with the name of Industrial Progress Company of Brazil (Companhia Progresso Industrial do Brasil). The factory was officially opened on 8 March 1893, in a complex with varying architectural styles like [Italianate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italianate_architecture), [Neo-Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture) and a tower in [Mansard Roof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansard_roof) style. After the opening in 1893, workers from [Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Britain) arrived in Bangu to work in the textile factory. The old farms became worker villages with red-brick houses, and a neo-Gothic church was created, which still exists as the Saint Sebastian and Saint Cecilia Parish Church. Street cinemas and cultural buildings also appeared. In May 1894, [Thomas Donohoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Donohoe), a British worker from [Busby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Busby,_East_Renfrewshire), Scotland, arrived in Bangu.[^cite_note-31]

Donohoe was amazed to discover that there was absolutely no knowledge of football among Brazilians. So he wrote to his wife, Elizabeth, asking her to bring a football when she joined him. And shortly after her arrival, in September 1894, the first football match in Brazil took place in the field beside the textile factory. It was a five-a-side match between British workers, and took place six months before the first game organized by [Charles Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_William_Miller) in São Paulo. However, the Bangu Football Club was not formally created until 1904.[^cite_note-32]

### Republican period



Rio de Janeiro, c. 1910s

At the time Brazil's [Old Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Brazilian_Republic) was established, the city lacked [urban planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_planning) and [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation), which helped spread several diseases, such as [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever), [dysentery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dysentery), [variola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variola), [tuberculosis](30653.md) and even [black death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_death). [Pereira Passos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Pereira_Passos), who was named mayor in 1902, imposed reforms to modernize the city, demolishing the [cortiços](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corti%C3%A7o) where most of the poor population lived. These people then moved to live in the city's hills, creating the first [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas).[^cite_note-33] Inspired by the city of [Paris](22989.md), Passos built the [Municipal Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatro_Municipal_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)), the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes) and the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil) in the city's center; brought [electric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_power) to Rio and created larger avenues to adapt the city to [automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automobiles).[^cite_note-34] Passos also named [Oswaldo Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswaldo_Cruz) as Director General of Public Health. Cruz's plans to clean the city of diseases included compulsory vaccination of the entire population and forced entry into houses to kill mosquitoes and rats. The people of the city rebelled against Cruz's policy, in what would be known as the [Vaccine Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_Revolt).[^cite_note-35]



[Carioca Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Aqueduct) in the 1920s

In 1910, Rio saw the [Revolt of the Lash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolt_of_the_Lash), where [Afro-Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian) crew members in the [Brazilian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Navy) mutinied against the heavy use of [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment), which was similar to the punishment enslaved people received. The mutineers took control of the [battleship *Minas Geraes*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_battleship_Minas_Geraes) and threatened to fire on the city. Another military revolt occurred in 1922, the [Copacabana Fort revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_Fort_revolt), a march against the Old Republic's [coronelism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronelism) and [café com leite politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9_com_leite_politics). This revolt marked the beginning of [Tenentism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenentism), a movement which resulted in the [Revolution of 1930](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_1930) that started the [Vargas Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vargas_Era).

Until the early years of the 20th century, the city was largely limited to the neighborhood now known as the historic city center (see below), on the mouth of Guanabara Bay. The city's center of gravity began to shift south and west to the so-called [Zona Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Zone_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) (South Zone) in the early part of the 20th century, when the first tunnel was built under the mountains between [Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo) and the neighborhood that is now known as [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)). Expansion of the city to the north and south was facilitated by the consolidation and electrification of Rio's streetcar transit system after 1905.[^cite_note-36] Botafogos natural environment, combined with the fame of the [Copacabana Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_Palace) Hotel, the luxury hotel of the Americas in the 1930s, helped Rio to gain the reputation it still holds today as a beach party town. This reputation has been somewhat tarnished in recent years by favela violence resulting from the narcotics trade and [militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_police_militias).[^cite_note-37]



A convoy of [M41 Walker Bulldog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M41_Walker_Bulldog) tanks along the streets of the city in 1968 during the [military rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_military_government). At the time, Rio de Janeiro was a city-state, capital of [Guanabara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_\(state\)).

Plans for moving the nation's capital city from Rio de Janeiro to the center of Brazil had been occasionally discussed, and when [Juscelino Kubitschek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juscelino_Kubitschek) was elected president in 1955, it was partially on the strength of promises to build a new capital.[^cite_note-38] Though many thought that it was just [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_campaign) rhetoric, Kubitschek managed to have Brasília and a new [Federal District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distrito_Federal_\(Brazil\)) built, at great cost, by 1960. On 21 April of that year, the capital of Brazil was officially moved to Brasília. The territory of the former Federal District became its own state, Guanabara, after the bay that borders it to the east, encompassing just the city of Rio de Janeiro. After the [1964 coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Brazilian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) that installed a [military dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_military_government), the [city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state) was the only state left in Brazil to oppose the military. Then, in 1975, a presidential decree known as "The Fusion" removed the city's federative status and merged it with the [State of Rio de Janeiro](229419.md), with the city of Rio de Janeiro replacing [Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niter%C3%B3i) as the state's capital, and establishing the [Rio de Janeiro Metropolitan Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro).[^cite_note-39]

In 1992, Rio hosted the [Earth Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_Summit), a [United Nations](31769.md) conference to fight [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_degradation). Twenty years later, in 2012, the city hosted another conference on [sustainable development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_development), named [United Nations Conference on Sustainable Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Conference_on_Sustainable_Development). The city hosted the [World Youth Day in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Youth_Day_2013), the second [World Youth Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Youth_Day) in [South America](26769.md) and first in Brazil. In the sports field, Rio de Janeiro was the host of the [2007 Pan American Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Pan_American_Games) and the [2014 FIFA World Cup](656933.md). On 2 October 2009, the [International Olympic Committee](15147.md) [announced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bids_for_the_2016_Summer_Olympics) that Rio de Janeiro would host the [2016 Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics) and the [2016 Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Paralympics), beating competitors [Chicago](6886.md), [Tokyo](30057.md), and [Madrid](41188263.md). The city became the first South American city to host the event and the second Latin American city (after [Mexico City](18987.md) in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Summer_Olympics)) to host the Games. Since the early 2010s, Rio de Janeiro has been inscribed as a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md) for its arts, urban culture and [designed landscapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Designed_landscape) set around a natural environment.

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Rio_de_Janeiro)



[Satellite](27683.md) view of [Greater Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro) at night seen from [ISS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISS)



Location in the state of Rio de Janeiro

Rio de Janeiro is near the west end of a strip (from [Cabo Frio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabo_Frio) to just east of [Ilha Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilha_Grande)) of Brazil's Atlantic coast close to the [Tropic of Capricorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Capricorn) where the shoreline is oriented east and west; the city thus faces largely south. It was founded at the entrance to an inlet, [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay) (Baía de Guanabara), which is marked by a point of land called Sugar Loaf (Pão de Açúcar) – a "calling card" of the city.[^cite_note-40]

The population of the city of Rio de Janeiro, occupying an area of 1,182.3 km<sup>2</sup> (456.5 sq mi),[^cite_note-41] is about 6,000,000.[^cite_note-42] The population of the greater metropolitan area is estimated at 11–13.5 million. Residents of the city are known as *[cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca)*. The official song of Rio is "[Cidade Maravilhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Maravilhosa)", by composer [André Filho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Filho).

### Parks



[Parque Lage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lage) with [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado) in the background



Arches in the [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Botanical_Garden)



[Rodrigo de Freitas Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_de_Freitas_Lagoon)

The city has parks and ecological reserves such as the Tijuca National Park, the world's first urban forest and [UNESCO](21786641.md) Environmental Heritage and Biosphere Reserve; [Pedra Branca State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_Branca_State_Park), which houses the highest point of Rio de Janeiro, the peak of Pedra Branca; the [Quinta da Boa Vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinta_da_Boa_Vista) complex; the [Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanical_Garden_of_Rio_de_Janeiro);[^cite_note-43] Rio's Zoo; [Parque Lage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lage); and the [Passeio Público](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passeio_P%C3%BAblico_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)), the first public park in the [Americas](29833.md).[^cite_note-44] In addition the [Flamengo Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamengo_Park) is the largest landfill in the city, extending from the center to the south zone, and containing museums and monuments, in addition to much vegetation.

Since 1961, the [Tijuca National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca_Forest) (Parque Nacional da Tijuca), the largest city-surrounded [urban forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_forest) and the second largest urban forest in the world, has been a National Park. The largest urban forest in the world is the Floresta da Pedra Branca (White Rock Forest), which is located in the West Zone of Rio de Janeiro.[^cite_note-45]

### Environment

Due to the high concentration of industries in the metropolitan region, the city has faced serious problems of environmental pollution. The [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay) has lost mangrove areas and suffers from residues from domestic and industrial sewage, oils, and heavy metals. Although its waters renew when they reach the sea, the bay is the final receiver of all the tributaries generated along its banks and in the basins of the many rivers and streams that flow into it. The levels of particulate matter in the air are twice as high as those recommended by the [World Health Organization](33583.md), in part because of the large numbers of vehicles in circulation.[^cite_note-46]

The waters of Sepetiba Bay are slowly following the path traced by Guanabara Bay, with sewage generated by a population of the order of 1.29 million inhabitants being released without treatment in streams or rivers. With regard to industrial pollution, highly toxic wastes, with high concentrations of heavy metals – mainly [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc) and [cadmium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadmium) – have been dumped over the years by factories in the industrial districts of [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Itaguaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itagua%C3%AD) and [Nova Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Igua%C3%A7u), constructed under the supervision of State policies.[^cite_note-OQ-47]

The Marapendi lagoon and the [Rodrigo de Freitas Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_de_Freitas_Lagoon) have suffered from the leniency of the authorities and the growth in the number of apartment buildings close by. The illegal discharge of sewage and the consequent deaths of algae diminished the oxygenation of the waters, causing fish mortality.[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49]

There are, on the other hand, signs of decontamination in the lagoon made through a [public-private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership) established in 2008 to ensure that the lagoon waters will eventually be suitable for bathing. The decontamination actions involve the transfer of sludge to large craters present in the lagoon itself, and the creation of a new direct and underground connection with the sea, which will contribute to increasing the daily water exchange between the two environments. However, during the Olympics the lagoon hosted the rowing competitions, and there were numerous concerns about potential infection resulting from human sewage.[^cite_note-50]



Panorama of the city of Rio de Janeiro highlighting the mountains of [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado) (left), [Sugarloaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain_\(Brazil\)) (center, background) and Two Brothers (right), from the [Chinese Belvedere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vista_Chinesa)



Elevated view from Morro Dois Irmãos over Leblon, Ipanema and Lagoa Rodrigo de Freitas, with the forested hills beyond. Morro do Cantagalo and the Cantagalo–Pavão-Pavãozinho communities appear in the centre, while Ipanema Beach and Arpoador extend to the right. In the distance are Pão de Açúcar, the entrance to Guanabara Bay, and the coastal hills and islands of Niterói.

### Climate

#### Overview



Night [storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storm) with [lightning](61344.md) in Rio de Janeiro, view from [Sugarloaf Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain)



[Copacabana Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_beach) on a sunny day

Rio has a [tropical wet and dry climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_wet_and_dry_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Aw*, [Trewartha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trewartha_climate_classification): *Awab* borderline *Awha*), and is often characterized by long periods of heavy rain between December and March.[^cite_note-51] The city experiences hot, humid summers, and warm, sunny winters. In inland areas of the city, temperatures above 40 °C (104 °F) are common during the summer, though rarely for long periods, while maximum temperatures above 27 °C (81 °F) occur regularly every month. Temperature varies according to elevation, distance from the coast, and type of vegetation or land use.

Summers are hot and stormy. Temperatures over 40 °C (104 °F) may happen throughout the warmer half-year but are much more common during the summer, often meaning the actual "feels-like" temperature is over 50 °C (122 °F), when there is little wind and the [relative humidity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_humidity) percentage is high.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55] Along the coast, the breeze, blowing onshore and offshore, moderates the temperature. Heavy rain is common in the summer, and these have, on some occasions, provoked catastrophic floods and landslides. The mountainous areas register greater rainfall since they constitute a barrier to the humid wind that comes from the Atlantic.[^cite_note-56]

Winters are warm and not as rainy. Cold fronts and dawn/morning sea breezes bring mild temperatures, though average temperatures remain above 25 °C (77 °F).

#### Notable meteorological events

Drought is rare, albeit bound to happen occasionally given the city's strongly seasonal tropical climate. The Brazilian drought of 2014–2015, most severe in the [Southeast Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Region,_Brazil) and the worst in decades, affected the entire metropolitan region's water supply (a diversion from the [Paraíba do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C3%ADba_do_Sul) River to the [Guandu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guandu_River_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) River is a major source for the state's most populous mesoregion). There were plans to divert the Paraíba do Sul to the [Sistema Cantareira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistema_Cantareira) (Cantareira system) during the water crisis of 2014 in order to help the critically drought-stricken [Greater São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_S%C3%A3o_Paulo) area. However, the availability of sufficient rainfall to supply tap water to both metropolitan areas in the future is merely speculative.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]



[Sugarloaf Cable Car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Cable_Car) approaching the summit

Some areas within [Rio de Janeiro state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_state) occasionally have falls of [snow grains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_grains) and [ice pellets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_pellets) (popularly called *granizo*) and [hail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail),[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62] and roughly in the same suburbs ([Nova Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Igua%C3%A7u) and surrounding areas, including parts of Campo Grande and Bangu) that correspond to the location of the March 2012, February–March 2013 and January 2015 ice pellet (*granizo*) falls, there was a [tornado](37530.md)-like phenomenon in January 2011, for the first time in the region's recorded history, causing structural damage and long-lasting [blackouts](296636.md), but no fatalities.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-9guaçutornado-64] The [World Meteorological Organization](33584.md) has advised that Brazil, especially its southeastern region, must be prepared for increasingly [severe weather](11593538.md) occurrences in the near future, since events such as the catastrophic [January 2011 Rio de Janeiro floods and mudslides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2011_Rio_de_Janeiro_floods_and_mudslides) are not an isolated phenomenon. In early May 2013, winds registering above 90 km/h (56 mph) caused blackouts in 15 neighborhoods of the city and three surrounding municipalities, and killed one person.[^cite_note-65] Rio saw similarly high winds (about 100 km/h (62 mph)) in January 2015.[^cite_note-66]

According to data from the Brazilian National Institute of Meteorology (INMET), since 1931, the absolute minimum temperature recorded in Rio de Janeiro was 6.4 °C on 18 August 1933,[^cite_note-NCB-1931-1960-67] at the meteorological station in the [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu,_Rio_de_Janeiro) neighborhood (deactivated in March 2004). This same station, located in the city's hottest neighborhood,[^cite_note-68] recorded a maximum temperature of 43.1 °C on 14 January 1984, which held the record for the highest temperature in the city until 26 December 2012, when 43.2 °C was recorded at the [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro) station.[^cite_note-69] The record for rainfall within 24 hours is 349.4 mm, recorded on 26 February 1971, at the former station in the [Engenho de Dentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engenho_de_Dentro) neighborhood.[^cite_note-bdmep.inmet.gov.br-70]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Brazilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilians), [Cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariocas), [Demographics of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Demographics of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Brazil), and [Immigration to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Brazil)

| Year | Pop. | ±% |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1872 | 274,972 | — |
| 1890 | 522,651 | +90.1% |
| 1900 | 811,443 | +55.3% |
| 1920 | 1,157,873 | +42.7% |
| 1940 | 1,764,141 | +52.4% |
| 1950 | 2,377,451 | +34.8% |
| 1960 | 3,281,908 | +38.0% |

| [^cite_note-92] |  |  |

According to the [2010 IBGE Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics), there were 5,940,224 people residing in the city of Rio de Janeiro.[^cite_note-93] Since 1960, when it was surpassed by [São Paulo](390875.md), the city of Rio de Janeiro has been the second-most populous city in Brazil.[^cite_note-94]

### Ethnic groups

The [2022 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_census) revealed the following numbers: [White Brazilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilian) (45.4% or 2,821,619); [Mixed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo_Brazilian) (38.7% or 2,403,895); [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian) (15.6% or 968,428); [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Brazilian) (10,514 or 0.2%); [Indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Brazilian) (6,531 or 0.1%).[^cite_note-95] The population of Rio de Janeiro was 53.2% [female](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female) and 46.8% [male](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Male).[^cite_note-autogenerated3-96]

The [black community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilian) was formed by residents whose ancestors had been trafficked into slavery, mostly from [Angola](701.md) and [Mozambique](19301.md), as well as by people of Angolan, Mozambican and [West African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_African) descent who moved to Rio from other parts of Brazil. Nearly half of the city's population is of mixed or black phenotype.[^cite_note-97] [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilian) in Brazil is defined more by having a European-looking phenotype rather than ancestry, and two full [siblings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sibling) can be of different "racial" categories[^cite_note-98] in a [skin color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_skin_color) and [phenotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) continuum from *pálido* (*branco*) or fair-skinned, through *branco [moreno](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/moreno)* or swarthy Caucasian, *mestiço claro* or lighter skinned multiracial, *pardo* (mixed race) to *negro* or [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people). [Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo), for example, in popular usage includes those who are *[caboclos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caboclo)* ([mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo)), *mulatos* ([mulattoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulattoes)), *[cafuzos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cafuzo)* ([zambos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambo)), *juçaras* (archaic term for [tri-racials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial#Brazil)) and westernized [Amerindians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Brazil) (which are called *caboclos* as well), being more of a skin color rather than a racial group in particular.

### Immigration and migration

Main article: [Immigration to Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Brazil)

| Ethnicity |  | Percentage |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Brazilians) |  | 45.4% |
| [Pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo) ([Multiracial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial#Brazil)) |  | 38.7% |
| [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians) |  | 15.6% |
| [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Brazilian) |  | 0.2% |
| [Amerindian](55510.md) |  | 0.1% |

Different ethnic groups contributed to the formation of the population of Rio de Janeiro. Before European colonization, there were at least seven different [indigenous peoples](55510.md) speaking 20 languages in the region. Some of them joined the Portuguese and the other the French. The Portuguese then exterminated those who joined the [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Brazilian), while the other part was assimilated.[^cite_note-99]

Rio de Janeiro is home to the largest [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian) population outside of [Lisbon](18091.md) in Portugal.[^cite_note-100] After independence from Portugal, Rio de Janeiro became a destination for hundreds of thousands of immigrants from Portugal, mainly in the early 20th century. The immigrants were mostly poor peasants who subsequently found prosperity in Rio as city workers and small traders.[^cite_note-101]



Portuguese immigrant in Rio de Janeiro, considered the largest "[Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian) city" outside Portugal

[^cite_note-auto1-102]

The Portuguese cultural influence is still seen in many parts of the city (and many other parts of the state of Rio de Janeiro), including architecture and [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language). Most Brazilians with some cultural contact with Rio know how to easily differentiate between the local dialect, *Carioca&#x20;*, and other Brazilian dialects. People of [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian) ancestry predominate in most of the state. The Brazilian census of 1920 showed that 39.7% of the Portuguese who lived in Brazil lived in Rio de Janeiro. Including all of Rio de Janeiro, the proportion rose to 46.3% of the Portuguese who lived in Brazil. The numerical presence of the Portuguese was extremely high, accounting for 72% of the foreigners who lived in the capital. Portuguese-born people accounted for 20.4% of the population of Rio, and those with a Portuguese father or a Portuguese mother accounted for 30.8%. In other words, native born Portuguese and their children accounted for 51.2% of the inhabitants of Rio, or a total of 267,664 people in 1890.[^cite_note-103]

| Group | Population | Percentage[^cite_note-104] |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Portuguese immigrants | 106,461 | 20.4% |
| Brazilians with at least one Portuguese parent | 161,203 | 30.8% |
| Portuguese immigrants and their descendants | 267,664 | 51.2% |

As a result of the influx of immigrants to Brazil from the late 19th to the early 20th century, also found in Rio de Janeiro and [its metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro) are communities of [Levantine Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Brazilian) who are mostly [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) or [Irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Brazil), [Spaniards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_immigration_to_Brazil), [Italians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Brazilian), [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Brazilian), [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Brazilians),[^cite_note-105]

| Genomic ancestry of non-related individuals in Rio de Janeiro[^cite_note-Suarez-Kurtz_Vargens_Self-reported_skin_color-106] |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Race or skin color | Number of individuals | Amerindian | African | European |
| White | 107 | 6.7% | 6.9% | 86.4% |
| *Pardo* (Mixed race) | 119 | 8.3% | 23.6% | 68.1% |
| Black | 109 | 7.3% | 50.9% | 41.8% |

According to an autosomal DNA study from 2009, conducted on a school in the poor suburb of Rio de Janeiro, the "pardos" there were found to be on average about 80% European, and the "whites" (who thought of themselves as "very mixed") were found to carry very little Amerindian and/or African admixtures. The results of the tests of genomic ancestry are quite different from the self-made estimates of European ancestry. In general, the test results showed that European ancestry is far more important than the students thought it would be. The "pardos" for example thought of themselves as 1⁄3 European, 1⁄3 African and 1⁄3 Amerindian before the tests, and yet their ancestry on average reached 80% European.[^cite_note-107] Other studies showed similar results[^cite_note-Suarez-Kurtz_Vargens_Self-reported_skin_color-106][^cite_note-108]

### Religion

See also: [Religion in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Brazil), [Protestantism in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Brazil), and [Roman Catholic Church in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church_in_Brazil)

| Religion |  | Percent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism) |  | 51.1% |
| [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) |  | 23.4% |
| Other Christian |  | 2.4% |
| [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) |  | 13.6% |
| [Spiritism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kardecist_spiritism) |  | 5.9% |
| Others |  | 3.6% |

Religion in Rio de Janeiro is diverse, with [Catholic Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Christianity) being the majority religion. According to data from the [Brazilian Institute of Geography and Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics) (IBGE), in 2010 the population of Rio de Janeiro had 3,229,192 [Roman Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Brazil) (51.1%), 1,477,021 [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Brazil) (23.4%), 372.851 [Spiritists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kardecist_spiritism) (5.9%), 37,974 [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses) (0.6%), 75,075 [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Brazil) (0.2%), 52,213 [Umbanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbanda) (0.8%), 21,800 [Jews](15624.md) (0.3%), 25,743 [Brazilian Catholic Apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Catholic_Apostolic_Church) (0.4%), 16,776 new eastern religious (0.2%), 28,843 [Candomblé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candombl%C3%A9) (0.4%), 3,853 [Mormons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormon) (<0.1%), 5,751 [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) Christians (<0.1%), 7,394 spiritualists (0.1%), 964 [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Brazil) (<0.1%), 5,662 esoteric (<0.1%) and 802 [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) (<0.1%). 858,704 had no religion (13.5%), and 113,530 followed other forms of Christianity (1.8%).[^cite_note-Religion-109]



*[Christ the Redeemer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_the_Redeemer_\(statue\))*

Rio de Janeiro has had a rich and influential Catholic tradition. The [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o_do_Rio_de_Janeiro) is the second largest archdiocese in Brazil after [São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_S%C3%A3o_Paulo).[^cite_note-110] The [Rio de Janeiro Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Cathedral) was inaugurated in 1979, in the central region of the city. Its installations have a collection of great historical and religious value: the Archdiocesan Museum of Sacred Art and the Archdiocesan Archive.[^cite_note-Religion2-111] In a [Contemporary architecture](3970948.md), it has a conical shape, with 96 meters of internal diameter and capacity to receive up to 20 thousand faithful. The splendor of the building, with straight and sober lines, is due to the changing stained glass windows carved on the walls up to the dome. Its design and execution was coordinated by [Monsignor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsignor) Ivo Antônio Calliari (1918–2005).[^cite_note-Religion2-111] [Saint Sebastian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Sebastian) is recognized as the city's [patron saint](68055.md), which is why it received the canonical name of "Saint Sebastian of Rio de Janeiro".[^cite_note-112]

Many [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) creeds coexist in the city, [Presbyterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian), [Congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_Churches), [Lutheran](23371382.md) and [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican) Churches, in addition to evangelical churches such as the [Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist), [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist), [Seventh-day Adventist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist), and [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal) churches, such as the [Universal Church of the Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Church_of_the_Kingdom_of_God), [Assembly of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_God), [Christian Congregation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Congregation_in_Brazil), and [The Foursquare Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Foursquare_Church).[^cite_note-Religion-109]

Afro-Brazilian religions such as [Umbanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbanda) and [Candomblé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candombl%C3%A9) find support in various social segments, although professed by less than 2% of the population, many Cariocas simultaneously observe those practices with Roman Catholicism.[^cite_note-Religion-109][^cite_note-113]

#### Christianity



[Presbyterian Cathedral of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Cathedral_of_Rio_de_Janeiro)

The [Roman Catholic Archdiocese of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_S%C3%A3o_Sebasti%C3%A3o_do_Rio_de_Janeiro), the Metropolitan See of its respective [Ecclesiastical Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_Province), belongs to the Regional Episcopal Council Leste I of the [National Conference of Bishops of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Conference_of_Bishops_of_Brazil) (CNBB) (headquartered in Rio until 1977). Founded in 1676, it covers a territory of 1,721 km<sup>2</sup>.[^cite_note-114]

The *[Cathedral of São Sebastião do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_St._Sebastian_of_Rio_de_Janeiro)*, or *Metropolitan Cathedral*, was inaugurated in 1979 in the [ central region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro,_Rio_de_Janeiro) of the city. Its facilities house a collection of great historical and religious value: the Archdiocesan Museum of Sacred Art and the Archdiocesan Archive. The Banco da Providência and the Archdiocesan [Caritas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caritas_Brazil) are also based there. In a contemporary architectural style, it has a conical shape, with an internal diameter of 96 meters and a capacity to hold up to 20,000 worshippers. The splendor of the building, with its straight and sober lines, is due to the changing [stained glass windows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stained_glass_windows) carved into the walls up to the [dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome). Its design and execution were coordinated by [Monsignor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsignor) [Ivo Antônio Calliari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Ant%C3%B4nio_Calliari?action=edit\&redlink=1) (1918–2005).[^cite_note-115]

The city is home to various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) or reformed denominations, exemplified by the [Presbyterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_of_Brazil), [Congregational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_church), [Lutheran](23371382.md), and [Anglican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican) churches. There are also evangelical churches such as the [Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist), [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist), [Seventh-day Adventist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist) churches, and those of [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism) origin: [Universal Church of the Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Church_of_the_Kingdom_of_God), [Assembly of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblies_of_God), [Christian Congregation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Congregation_in_Brazil), [Foursquare Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foursquare_Church), [House of Blessing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabernacle_Evangelical_Church_of_Jesus?action=edit\&redlink=1), [God is Love Pentecostal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_is_Love_Pentecostal_Church), [Christian Maranatha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maranatha_Christian_Church?action=edit\&redlink=1), and New Life.[^cite_note-sidra1-116]

### Education

Main article: [List of Rio de Janeiro schools, colleges, universities and research centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Rio_de_Janeiro_schools,_colleges,_universities_and_research_centers)



Center for [Human Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_science) of the [Federal University of the State of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_the_State_of_Rio_de_Janeiro)

The [Portuguese language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language) is the official and [national language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language), and thus the primary language taught in schools. [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) and [Spanish](26825.md) are also part of the official curriculum. There are also international schools, such as the [American School of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_School_of_Rio_de_Janeiro), Our Lady of Mercy School, SIS Swiss International School, the Corcovado German School, the Lycée Français and the British School of Rio de Janeiro.[^cite_note-117]

The city has several universities and research institutes. The [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Brazil\)) has certified approximately 99 upper-learning institutions in Rio.[^cite_note-118] The most prestigious university is the [Federal University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro). It is the fifth-best in Latin America; the second-best in Brazil, second only to the [University of São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_S%C3%A3o_Paulo); and the best in [Latin America](18524.md), according to the [QS World University Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]

Some notable higher education institutions are [Federal University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) (UFRJ); [Federal University of the State of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidade_Federal_do_Estado_do_Rio_de_Janeiro) (UNIRIO); [Rio de Janeiro State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_University) (UERJ); [Federal Rural University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidade_Federal_Rural_do_Rio_de_Janeiro) (UFRRJ, often nicknamed *Rural*); [Fluminense Federal University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluminense_Federal_University) (UFF); [Pontifical Catholic University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Catholic_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) (PUC-Rio); [Getúlio Vargas Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funda%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Get%C3%BAlio_Vargas) (FGV); [Military Institute of Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Militar_de_Engenharia) (IME); [Superior Institute of Technology in Computer Science of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Superior_de_Tecnologia_em_Ci%C3%AAncias_da_Computa%C3%A7%C3%A3o_do_Rio_de_Janeiro) (IST-Rio); College of Publicity and Marketing (ESPM); [The Brazilian Center for Research in Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Brasileiro_de_Pesquisas_F%C3%ADsicas) (CBPF); [National Institute of Pure and Applied Mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Matem%C3%A1tica_Pura_e_Aplicada) (IMPA); Superior institute of Education of Rio de Janeiro (ISERJ) and [Federal Center of Technological Education Celso Suckow da Fonseca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Federal_de_Educa%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Tecnol%C3%B3gica_Celso_Suckow_da_Fonseca) (CEFET/RJ). There are more than 137 upper-learning institutions in whole Rio de Janeiro state.[^cite_note-121]



Superior institute of Education of Rio de Janeiro (ISERJ)

The [Rio de Janeiro State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_University) (public), [Federal University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) (public), Brazilian Institute of Capital Markets (private) and [Pontifical Catholic University of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Catholic_University_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) (private) are among the country's top institutions of higher education. Other institutes of higher learning include the Colégio Regina Coeli in Usina, notable for having its own [3 ft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3_ft_gauge_railways) (914 mm) [narrow-gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow-gauge_railway)[^cite_note-122] [funicular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funicular) railway on its grounds.[^cite_note-123]

Primary schools are largely under municipal administration, while the state plays a more significant role in the extensive network of secondary schools. There are also a small number of schools under federal administration, such as Pedro II School, Colégio de Aplicação da UFRJ, and the Centro Federal de Educação Tecnológica of Rio de Janeiro (CEFET-RJ). In addition, Rio has an ample offering of private schools that provide education at all levels. Rio is home to many colleges and universities. The literacy rate for [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca) aged 10 and older is nearly 95 percent, well above the national average.[^cite_note-124] In Rio, there were 1,033 primary schools with 25,594 teachers and 667,788 students in 1995. There are 370 secondary schools with 9,699 teachers and 227,892 students. There are 53 University-preparatory schools with 14,864 teachers and 154,447 students. The city has six major universities and 47 private schools of higher learning.[^cite_note-125]

### Social issues

Main articles: [Social issues in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_issues_in_Brazil), [Social apartheid in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_apartheid_in_Brazil), and [Favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela)



[Rocinha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocinha), the largest [slum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum) (*[favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela)*) in Brazil

There are significant disparities between the rich and the poor in Rio de Janeiro, and different socioeconomic groups are largely segregated into different neighborhoods.[^cite_note-126] Although the city clearly ranks among the world's major metropolises, large numbers live in slums known as [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela), where 95% of the population are poor, compared to 40% in the general population.[^cite_note-127]

There have been a number of government initiatives to counter this problem, from the removal of the population from favelas to [housing projects](177912.md) such as [Cidade de Deus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_de_Deus_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) to the more recent approach of improving conditions in the favelas and bringing them up to par with the rest of the city, as was the focus of the "Favela Bairro" program and deployment of [Pacifying Police Units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifying_Police_Unit).

Rio has the second highest population of favela residents in Brazil, but the 11th highest proportion of its total population living in favelas.[^cite_note-128] More than 1.3 million people live in its 813 favelas, 21% of Rio's total population.[^cite_note-129]

Rio also has a large proportion of state-sanctioned violence, with about 20% of all killings committed by state security.[^cite_note-130] In 2019, police killed an average of five people each day in the state of Rio de Janeiro, with a total of 1,810 killed in the year. This was more police killings than any year since official records started in 1998.[^cite_note-131]

## Politics

### Municipal government



São Sebastião Administrative Center, seat of City Hall



[Pedro Ernesto Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Ernesto_Palace), in [Cinelândia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinel%C3%A2ndia), seat of [Municipal Council](22849342.md)

In Rio de Janeiro, the executive power is represented by the mayor and the cabinet of secretaries, in accordance with the model proposed by the Federal Constitution. The Organic Law of the Municipality and the current Master Plan, however, stipulate that the public administration must provide the population with effective tools for the exercise of participatory democracy. In this way, the city is divided into subprefectures, each of which is headed by a sub-mayor appointed directly by the mayor.[^cite_note-132]

Legislative power is constituted by the municipal council, composed of 51 councilors[^cite_note-133] elected for four-year terms (in compliance with the provisions of article 29 of the Constitution, which disciplines a minimum number of 42 and a maximum of 55 for municipalities with more than five million inhabitants).[^cite_note-134] It is up to the House to prepare and vote on fundamental laws for the administration and the Executive, especially the participatory budget (Lei de Diretrizes Orçamentárias). Although the veto power is granted to the mayor, the process of voting on the laws that oppose him usually generates conflicts between the Executive and the Legislative.[^cite_note-135]

There are also municipal councils, which complement the legislative process and the work engendered in the secretariats. Compulsorily formed by representatives of various sectors of organized civil society, they are on different fronts — although their effective representation is sometimes questioned. The following are currently in operation: Municipal Council for the Protection of Cultural Heritage (CMPC), Defense of the Environment (CONDEMAM), Health (CMS), the Rights of Children and Adolescents (CMDCA), Education (CME), Social Assistance (CMAS) and Anti-Drugs.[^cite_note-136]

### State government



[Guanabara Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Palace), seat of the state government



[Tiradentes Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiradentes_Palace), seat of the [Legislative Assembly](39255501.md)

As the capital of the homonymous state, the city is the seat of the state government. The [Guanabara Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Palace) (formerly known as Paço Isabel) is located in the Laranjeiras neighborhood, in the south zone, and is the official seat of the Rio de Janeiro executive power. Not to be confused with Palácio Laranjeiras, situated in the same neighborhood, which is the official residence of the governor of Rio de Janeiro.[^cite_note-hist-137]

The [Legislative Assembly of Rio de Janeiro](39255501.md) (ALERJ) is the state legislative body[^cite_note-138] and is headquartered at [Tiradentes Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiradentes_Palace),[^cite_note-139] where the [Chamber of Deputies of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Brazil) previously functioned.[^cite_note-Acervo_O_Globo-140]

The Court of Justice of the State of Rio de Janeiro (TJRJ) is the highest body of the judiciary in the state. Its central court is located in downtown Rio de Janeiro, but, from 2013 to July 2018, some of the courts of this court were moved to Cidade Nova.[^cite_note-141]

### Federal government

The city of Rio de Janeiro was successively the capital of the Portuguese colony of the [State of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Brazil) (1621–1815), after the [United Kingdom of Portugal, Brazil and the Algarves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Portugal,_Brazil_and_the_Algarves) (1815–1822), the [Empire of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil) (1822–1889) and from the Republic of the United States of Brazil (1889–1968) until 1960, when the seat of government was definitively transferred to the then newly built [Brasília](4752.md).[^cite_note-nexo-142]

Despite the change in the federal capital, 59% of civil servants in the Executive Branch of federal agencies and public companies remained in the city. Rio de Janeiro is also the only Brazilian state where the number of federal employees exceeds the number of state employees. About a third of all federal public bodies and companies remain in the former capital, with 50 public offices, including agencies, autarchies, foundations and public companies, such as the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil), the [National Nuclear Energy Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Nuclear_Energy_Commission), [Fiocruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiocruz), [BNDES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BNDES), [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras), [Eletrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobras), [IBGE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBGE), [Casa da Moeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_da_Moeda_do_Brasil), the [National Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_National_Archives), among others.[^cite_note-nexo-142]

### Subdivisions



Municipality of Rio de Janeiro and its division into zones and neighborhoods&#x20;

| West Zone | North Zone | South Zone | Central Zone |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

The city is commonly divided into the historic center (Centro); the tourist-friendly wealthier South Zone (Zona Sul); the residential less wealthy North Zone (Zona Norte); peripheries in the West Zone (Zona Oeste), among them [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and the wealthy newer [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca) district. Rio de Janeiro is administratively divided into 33 *distritos* (districts) named *Regiões Administrativas* ("Administrative Regions") and 166 *bairros* (neighborhoods).[^cite_note-143]

Subprefectures are officially grouped into four regions (or "zones"), taking into account geographic position and occupation history; however, these do not have any administrative or political power over the municipality. The official political division of the municipality takes into account historical-cultural characteristics to divide the neighborhoods.[^cite_note-\:0-144] Most of its population is concentrated in the neighborhoods of Campo Grande, Santa Cruz, Bangu, Tijuca, Realengo, Jacarepaguá, Copacabana, Barra da Tijuca, Maré, Guaratiba and Taquara together, these eleven neighborhoods concentrate a population of 1.5 million inhabitants, according to the 2010 census.[^cite_note-145]

[*Centro* or Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro) is the historic core of the city, as well as its financial and commercial center. Sites of interest include the [Paço Imperial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pa%C3%A7o_Imperial), built during colonial times to serve as a residence for the Portuguese governors of Brazil; many historic churches, such as the [Candelária Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candel%C3%A1ria_Church) (the former cathedral), São José, Santa Lucia, Nossa Senhora do Carmo, Santa Rita, São Francisco de Paula, and the monasteries of Santo Antônio and São Bento. The Centro also houses the modern concrete [Rio de Janeiro Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Cathedral). Around the [Cinelândia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinel%C3%A2ndia) square, there are several landmarks of the [*Belle Époque* of Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Belle_%C3%89poque), such as the [Municipal Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Municipal_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) and the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil) building. Among its several museums, the [Museu Nacional de Belas Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes) (National Museum of Fine Arts) and the [Museu Histórico Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historical_Museum_\(Brazil\)) (National Historical Museum) are the most important.

The Cultural Corridor in Rio's City Center is home to one of the most architecturally and historically preserved districts, known as the Sociedade de Amigos das Adjacências da Rua da Alfândega (SAARA). This association was formed by merchants operating near Rua da Alfândega in the Historic Center of Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. The area is renowned for its stores specializing in popular home goods and fabrics, housed within several historic buildings. The name comes from the occupation of the region by Muslim, Jewish, and Maronite Christian immigrants from the Ottoman Empire in the early 20th century, for commercial purposes. The region preserves examples of various architectural styles, from Portuguese colonial, through Neoclassical, Eclectic, Art Deco, and Modernism, making the region a rich tapestry of historical architecture that refers to the city's colonial and imperial past.



Aerial view of [Fort Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Copacabana), with [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) (right) and [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema) (left, background)



[Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca) with [Pedra da Gávea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_da_G%C3%A1vea) in background

The [South Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Zone_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) (*Zona Sul*) is composed of several districts, among which are [São Conrado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Conrado), [Leblon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leblon), [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema), [Arpoador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arpoador), [Copacabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)), and [Leme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leme_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)), which compose Rio's Atlantic beach coastline.[^cite_note-146] The neighborhood of Copacabana beach hosts one of the world's most spectacular New Year's Eve parties ("Reveillon"), as more than two million revelers crowd onto the sands to watch the [fireworks display](59493.md). From 2001, the fireworks have been launched from boats, to improve the safety of the event.[^cite_note-147]

The North Zone (*Zona Norte*) begins at Grande Tijuca (the middle class residential and commercial *bairro* of [Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca)), just west of the city center, and sprawls for miles inland until [Baixada Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baixada_Fluminense) and the city's Northwest.[^cite_note-148] This region is home to the Maracanã (located in Grande Tijuca), once the world's highest capacity [football](10568.md) venue, able to hold nearly 199,854 people,[^cite_note-149] as it did for the [World Cup](11370.md) [final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguay_v_Brazil_\(1950_FIFA_World_Cup\)) of [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup). This region is also home to most of the [samba schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba_school) of Rio de Janeiro, such as Mangueira, Salgueiro, Império Serrano, Unidos da Tijuca, Imperatriz Leopoldinense, among others. Some of the main neighborhoods of Rio's North Zone are Alto da Boa Vista, which shares the [Tijuca Rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca_Forest) with the South and Southwest Zones; Tijuca, Vila Isabel, Méier, [São Cristovão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Crist%C3%B3v%C3%A3o_\(Rio_de_Janeiro_neighborhood\)), Madureira, Penha, Manguinhos, Fundão, Olaria, among others. Many of Rio de Janeiro's [slums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slums) (*[favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas)*) are located in the North Zone.[^cite_note-150]

West Zone (*Zona Oeste*) of Rio de Janeiro is a vaguely defined area that covers some 50% of the city's entire area, including Barra da Tijuca and Recreio dos Bandeirantes neighborhoods. The West Side of Rio has many historic sites because of the old "Royal Road of Santa Cruz" that crossed the territory in the regions of Realengo, Bangu, and Campo Grande, finishing at the Royal Palace of Santa Cruz in the Santa Cruz region. The highest peak of the city of Rio de Janeiro is the Pedra Branca Peak (Pico da Pedra Branca) inside the [Pedra Branca State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_Branca_State_Park). It has an altitude of 1024m. The Pedra Branca State Park (Parque Estadual da Pedra Branca)[^cite_note-151] is the biggest urban state park in the world comprising 17 neighborhoods in the west side, being a "giant lung" in the city with trails,[^cite_note-152] waterfalls and historic constructions like an old aqueduct in the Colônia Juliano Moreira[^cite_note-153] in the neighborhood of [Taquara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taquara,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and a dam in [Camorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camorim). Santa Cruz and Campo Grande Region have exhibited economic growth, mainly in the [Campo Grande neighborhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro). Industrial enterprises are being built in lower and lower middle class residential [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro), one of the largest and most populous of Rio de Janeiro's neighborhoods, most notably [Ternium Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternium), a new steel mill with its own private docks on Sepetiba Bay, which is planned to be South America's largest steel works.[^cite_note-154] A tunnel called *Túnel da Grota Funda*, opened in 2012, creating a [public transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transit) facility between Barra da Tijuca and Santa Cruz, lessening travel time to the region from other areas of Rio de Janeiro.[^cite_note-VejaRio2011-155]

On 9 September 2025, many [neighbourhoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighbourhood) that comprise the former West Zone were set apart to create the Southwest Zone (*Zona Sudoeste*). The Southwest Zone is compound by [Anil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anil,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca), [Camorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camorim), [Cidade de Deus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_de_Deus,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Curicica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curicica), [Freguesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freguesia_\(Jacarepagu%C3%A1\)), [Gardênia Azul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gard%C3%AAnia_Azul), [Grumari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumari), [Itanhangá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itanhang%C3%A1,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepagu%C3%A1), [Joá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A1), [Praça Seca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%C3%A7a_Seca), [Pechincha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechincha), [Rio das Pedras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_das_Pedras,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Recreio dos Bandeirantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recreio_dos_Bandeirantes), [Tanque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanque), [Taquara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taquara,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Vargem Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vargem_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Vargem Pequena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vargem_Pequena) and [Vila Valqueire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vila_Valqueire).[^cite_note-156]

### Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities

Rio de Janeiro is a part of the [Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Ibero-American_Capital_Cities).[^cite_note-200]

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) and [Economy of Brazil](3634.md)



[Downtown Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)), in the financial district of the city



[Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo) with the [Sugarloaf Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain_\(Brazil\))



Largo da Carioca, in Downtown Rio



[Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca)

Rio de Janeiro has the second-largest [GDP](12594.md) of any city in Brazil, surpassed only by [São Paulo](390875.md). According to the [IBGE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Geography_and_Statistics), it was approximately US\$201 billion in 2008, equivalent to 5.1% of the national total. Taking into consideration the network of influence exerted by the urban [metropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolis) (which covers 11.3% of the population), this share in GDP rises to 14.4%, according to a study released in October 2008 by the IBGE.[^cite_note-201]

Greater Rio de Janeiro, as perceived by the IBGE, has a GDP of US\$187 billion, constituting the second largest hub of national wealth. Per capita GDP is US\$11,786.[^cite_note-202] It concentrates 68% of the state's economic strength and 7.9% of all goods and services produced in the country.[^cite_note-203] The services sector comprises the largest portion of GDP (65.5%), followed by commerce (23.4%), industrial activities (11.1%) and agriculture (0.1%).[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205]

Benefiting from the federal capital position it had for a long period (1763–1960), the city became a dynamic administrative, financial, commercial, and cultural center. Rio de Janeiro became an attractive place for companies to locate when it was the capital of Brazil, as important sectors of society and of the government were present in the city. The city was chosen as headquarters for [state-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_property) companies such as [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras), [Eletrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobras), [Caixa Econômica Federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caixa_Econ%C3%B4mica_Federal), [National Economic and Social Development Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Development_Bank) and [Vale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vale_\(mining_company\)) (which was privatized in the 1990s). The [Rio de Janeiro Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Stock_Exchange) (BVRJ), which currently trades only government securities, was the first stock exchange founded in Brazil in 1845.

The offshore oil exploration in the [Campos Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campos_Basin) began in 1968 and became the main site for oil production in Brazil.
