This caused many oil and gas companies to be based in Rio de Janeiro, such as the Brazilian branches of [Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Dutch_Shell), [EBX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBX_Group) and [Esso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esso). For many years Rio was the second largest industrial hub of Brazil,[^cite_note-206] with [oil refineries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_refineries), shipbuilding industries, steel, [metallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallurgy), petrochemicals, cement, [pharmaceutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutic), textile, processed foods and furniture industries.

Major international pharmaceutical companies have their Brazilian headquarters in Rio, such as: [Merck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merck_KGaA), [Roche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche_Applied_Science), Arrow, Darrow, [Baxter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baxter_International), Mayne, and Mappel.

Recent decades have seen a sharp transformation in its economic profile, which is becoming more and more one of a major national hub of services and businesses.[^cite_note-207] The city is the headquarters of large telecom companies, such as [Intelig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelig_Telecom), [Oi](1441156.md) and [Embratel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embratel). Major Brazilian entertainment and media organizations are based in Rio de Janeiro, such as [Organizações Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organiza%C3%A7%C3%B5es_Globo), and also some of Brazil's major newspapers: *[Jornal do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jornal_do_Brasil)*, *[O Dia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Dia)*, and *Business Rio*.

Tourism and entertainment are other key aspects of the city's economic life. The city is the nation's top tourist attraction for both Brazilians and foreigners.[^cite_note-208]

In Greater Rio, which has one of the highest per capita incomes in Brazil, retail trade is substantial. Many of the most important retail stores are located in the center. Still, others are scattered throughout the commercial areas of the other districts, where shopping centers, supermarkets, and other retail businesses handle a large volume of consumer trade.[^cite_note-209]

Rio de Janeiro is (as of 2014) the second largest exporting municipality in Brazil. Annually, Rio exported a total of US\$7.49B worth of goods.[^cite_note-210] The top three goods exported by the municipality were [crude petroleum](23195.md) (40%), [semi-finished iron products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-finished_casting_products) (16%), and semi-finished steel products (11%).[^cite_note-211] Material categories of mineral products (42%) and metals (29%) make up 71% of all exports from Rio.[^cite_note-212]

Compared to other cities, Rio de Janeiro's economy is the second-largest in Brazil, behind [São Paulo](390875.md), and the [30th largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP) with a GDP of [R\$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Real) 201,9 billion in 2010. The per capita income for the city was R\$22,903 in 2007 (around US\$14,630).[^cite_note-213] Largely because of the strength of Brazil's currency at the time, [Mercer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_\(consulting_firm\))'s [city rankings of cost of living for expatriate employees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_expensive_cities_for_expatriate_employees#Mercer_surveys), reported that Rio de Janeiro ranked 12th among the most expensive cities in the world in 2011, up from the 29th position in 2010, just behind São Paulo (ranked 10th), and ahead of London, Paris, [Milan](36511.md), and New York.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215] Rio also had the most expensive hotel rates in Brazil, and the daily rate of its [five star hotels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_star_hotel) were the second most expensive in the world after only New York.[^cite_note-216]

### Highlighted sectors



Headquarters building of [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras)

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(August 2024)* |
| --- | --- |

Rio de Janeiro is home to many of Brazil's largest business conglomerates. Among them are the three largest multinationals in the energy and mining sectors: [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras), [Vale S.A.](1873300.md), and the [EBX Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBX_Group); the largest media and communications group in Latin America, [Grupo Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_Globo); and major telecommunications companies like [CorpCo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CorpCo?action=edit\&redlink=1) (owner of [Oi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oi_\(company\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Portugal Telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portugal_Telecom)), [TIM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TIM_Brasil), [Embratel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embratel), [Intelig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelig?action=edit\&redlink=1), and [Star One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embratel_Star_One) (the largest satellite management company in Latin America).[^cite_note-217]

In the petrochemical sector, there are more than 700 companies, including Brazil's largest ([Shell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shell_plc), [Esso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esso), [Ipiranga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipiranga_\(company\)), [Chevron](284749.md), [PRIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PRIO_S.A.), [Repsol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repsol)). Most maintain research centers throughout the state and together produce over four-fifths of the petroleum and fuels distributed at service stations nationwide. The [Companhia Siderúrgica Nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companhia_Sider%C3%BArgica_Nacional) (CSN),[^cite_note-218] [Ternium Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternium_Brasil?action=edit\&redlink=1)[^cite_note-219] (the largest steel mill in Latin America[^cite_note-220]) and the Brazilian branch of [BHP Billiton](200825.md)[^cite_note-221] play significant roles in the mining sector. The city also hosts the main national and international groups of the [shipbuilding industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipbuilding_industry) and the largest shipyards in the state and throughout Brazil, which produce about 90% of the ships and [offshore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_construction) equipment in Brazil.[^cite_note-222]

[Americanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americanas), [Coca-Cola Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca-Cola_Brasil?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Eletrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eletrobras), [Michelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin), [Neoenergia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoenergia?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Xerox do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerox), GE Oil & Gas, [Light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_S/A?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Chemtech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemtech?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Transpetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpetro), [BAT Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAT_Brasil?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Grupo SulAmérica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SulAm%C3%A9rica_Seguros) and [Vibra Energia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vibra_Energia) are among the major companies headquartered in the city. The city has a significant number of pharmaceutical industries, including [Schering-Plough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schering-Plough),[^cite_note-223] [GlaxoSmithKline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GlaxoSmithKline),[^cite_note-224] [Roche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoffmann%E2%80%93La_Roche),[^cite_note-225] and [Merck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merck_KGaA).[^cite_note-226]



Replica of the [Ganges River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_River) at the scenic city of [Projac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Projac), the second-largest television complex in [Latin America](18524.md)

[^cite_note-227]

Rio de Janeiro has inherited a strong cultural vocation from its past. It currently hosts the main production centers of Brazilian television: the [Estúdios Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Est%C3%BAdios_Globo) of [TV Globo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Globo), the [Casablanca Estúdios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casablanca_Est%C3%BAdios?action=edit\&redlink=1) of [Record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Record_\(TV_network\)), and the *Polo de Cinema de Jacarepaguá* — responsible for creating about 10,000 direct jobs and 30,000 indirect jobs. In 2006, 65% of Brazilian cinema production was done exclusively by studios in Rio, generating 91 million reais in federal funds through [tax incentive laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rouanet_Law). A significant part of the Brazilian editorial graphic industry is also present. In the phonographic industry, companies such as [EMI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EMI),[^cite_note-228] [Universal Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Music_Group),[^cite_note-229] [Sony Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sony_Music),[^cite_note-230] [Warner Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warner_Music_Brasil)[^cite_note-231] and [Som Livre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Som_Livre) are present.[^cite_note-232]

Many state-owned companies, public foundations, and federal autarchies have their headquarters in the city, including the [National Bank for Economic and Social Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bank_for_Economic_and_Social_Development) (BNDES),[^cite_note-233] the [Casa da Moeda do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casa_da_Moeda_do_Brasil),[^cite_note-234] the [Indústrias Nucleares do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ind%C3%BAstrias_Nucleares_do_Brasil?action=edit\&redlink=1) (INB),[^cite_note-235] the [Financiadora de Estudos e Projetos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financiadora_de_Estudos_e_Projetos) (FINEP),[^cite_note-236] the [Instituto Brasileiro de Geografia e Estatística](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Brasileiro_de_Geografia_e_Estat%C3%ADstica) (IBGE),[^cite_note-237] the [Instituto Nacional de Metrologia, Normalização e Qualidade Industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_de_Metrologia,_Qualidade_e_Tecnologia) (Inmetro),[^cite_note-238] the [Instituto Nacional da Propriedade Industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_da_Propriedade_Industrial_\(Brasil\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) (INPI),[^cite_note-239] the [Comissão de Valores Mobiliários](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comiss%C3%A3o_de_Valores_Mobili%C3%A1rios) (CVM),[^cite_note-240] and the [Comissão Nacional de Energia Nuclear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comiss%C3%A3o_Nacional_de_Energia_Nuclear) (CNEN).[^cite_note-241]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Brazil](8794264.md)



[Ipanema beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema_beach)



[Museum of Tomorrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Tomorrow)

Rio de Janeiro is Brazil's primary tourist attraction and resort. It receives the most visitors per year of any city in South America, with 2.82 million international tourists a year.[^cite_note-242]

Attractions in the city include approximately 80 kilometers of beaches, [Corcovado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corcovado) and [Sugarloaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarloaf_Mountain_\(Brazil\)) mountains, and the [Maracanã Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracan%C3%A3_Stadium). While the city had in the past had a thriving tourism sector, the industry entered a decline in the last quarter of the 20th century. Annual international airport arrivals dropped from 621,000 to 378,000, and average hotel occupancy dropped to 50% between 1985 and 1993.[^cite_note-243] The fact that [Brasília](4752.md) replaced Rio de Janeiro as the Brazilian capital in 1960 and that [São Paulo](390875.md) replaced Rio as the country's commercial, financial and main cultural center during the mid-20th century, has also been cited as a leading cause of the decline.[^cite_note-Worldwide_destinations-244]

Rio de Janeiro's government has since undertaken to modernize the city's economy, reduce its chronic social inequalities, and improve its commercial standing as part of an initiative for the regeneration of the tourism industry.[^cite_note-Worldwide_destinations-244]

Rio de Janeiro is an international hub of active and diverse [nightlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife) with [bars](272207.md), dance bars and [nightclubs](19321330.md) staying open well past midnight.[^cite_note-245] The city is a global [LGBTQ destination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_tourism), with 1 million LGBT tourists visiting each year.[^cite_note-246]

Farme de Amoedo Street (*Rua Farme de Amoedo*) is located in [Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipanema). The street and the nearby beach are popular in the [LGBTQ community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_community).[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248]

## Infrastructure

### Public transportation



Public transport map of Rio de Janeiro

Rio de Janeiro's [public transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transportation) modes include [city buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_bus), [coach buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_\(bus\)), [BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_Rapid_Transit), [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit), [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_rail), [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail), [ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries), [inclined elevators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclined_elevator), [vans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_taxi) and a [cable car](298681.md). Collectively, they carry over 2.6 million passengers per day.[^cite_note-General_public_transport_passenger_numbers_within_municipality-249] The high [fares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fares) on Rio's public transportation have significantly contributed to the high levels of [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_inequality) and [economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality) inequality in the city[^cite_note-250] and were the main motive of the [2013 protest movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_protests_in_Brazil).[^cite_note-251] Although there is limited fare integration through the [RioCard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RioCard), Rio de Janeiro's fare systems are still disjointed and not fully accessible.[^cite_note-252]

In 2022, the average amount of time people spent commuting with public transit in Rio de Janeiro on a weekday was 67 minutes. 12% of public transit riders had commutes that took more than two hours per day. The average amount of time people waited at a stop or station for public transit was 21 minutes, with 12% of riders waiting less than 5 minutes and 41% of riders waiting for over 20 minutes. The average distance people usually traveled in a single trip with public transit was 11.4 km (7.1 mi). The average distance people walked on their commute was 634 m (0.39 miles). 11% of people made exactly two transfers during their commute, and 2% of commuters made 3 or more transfers.[^cite_note-253]

#### Buses and vans

Main article: [Bus transport in Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_transport_in_Rio_de_Janeiro)



[City bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_bus) in mixed traffic



[Transcarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcarioca) Bus Rapid Transit (BRT)



[Van](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_taxi)



[Coach bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_bus) used for local intracity service

As of 2023, there are 354 municipal bus lines serving over 1.75 million passengers per day, as well as intercity lines.[^cite_note-Bus_fleet_numbers_within_municipality-254] The city has 20 [bus lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_lanes).[^cite_note-255] Most conventional bus routes are operated by 28 private companies divided into 4 [consortiums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consortiums), though 3 routes are run by the city government.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-MOBI-Rio-257] Although most buses are [conventional city buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_bus), there are also a handful of [coach buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coach_bus) used for local intracity service with more expensive fares.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262]

The [Bus Rapid Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_Rapid_Transit) system consists of 31 lines running along 4 corridors with [a wide variety of service patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-stop): [Transbrasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransBrasil_\(bus_rapid_transit\)), [Transcarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcarioca), [Transolímpica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transol%C3%ADmpica) and [Transoeste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transoeste). The municipal government runs the system through Mobi-Rio, which is a [state-owned enterprise](204768.md).[^cite_note-MOBI-Rio-257] It is 157 km (98 mi) long and has 153 stations as well as 731 buses that carry 535,000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

In addition to buses, Rio de Janeiro also has an extensive system of [vans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Share_taxi) used for public transport. This mode can more effectively traverse the steep, narrow and winding roads common in many [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas) than buses and is often a lifeline for them.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] However, most vans are run by [criminal organizations](22625.md), particularly [police militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_police_militias), which use their fares as a major source of revenue: Of the approximately 10,000 vans used for public transport in the city, only 2,000 are legal. The 2,000 legal vans carry approximately 310,000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270]

Rio de Janeiro also has [intercity, interstate and international bus lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_bus_service). Most long-distance bus lines run to [Novo Rio Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo_Rio_Bus_Terminal) at the edge of the [Zona Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro).[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] Long-distance buses also run to [bus stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_stations) in [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro)[^cite_note-273] and [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca).[^cite_note-274]

#### Urban rail

Main articles: [Rio de Janeiro Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Metro), [SuperVia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SuperVia), [Rio de Janeiro Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Light_Rail), and [Santa Teresa Tram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Teresa_Tram)



Train of Rio de Janeiro Metro



Urban train from SuperVia



Rio de Janeiro Light Rail



Santa Teresa Tram

Rio de Janeiro nominally has three [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) lines, though two of them (Lines [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_1_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) and [4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_4_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\))) are operationally run as a single line.[^cite_note-275] The system runs on 58 km (36 mi) of track and has 41 stations.[^cite_note-276] Line 1/4 runs from [Uruguai/Tijuca station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguai_Station) in [Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tijuca) to [Jardim Oceânico/Barra da Tijuca station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jardim_Oce%C3%A2nico_Station) in [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca); [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_2_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) runs from [Pavuna station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavuna_Station) in [Pavuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavuna) to [Botafogo station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo_Station) in [Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo). Line 1/4 and Line 2 share tracks between [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil) and Botafogo. The metro system carries 651,000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers) per day.[^cite_note-277]

The city also has a [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_rail) system operated by [SuperVia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SuperVia) that connects the city of Rio with other locations in [Greater Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Rio_de_Janeiro). It has 8 lines on 270 km (168 mi) of track with 103 stations in 12 [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_municipalities); Rio de Janeiro municipality itself has 127 km (79 mi) of track with 59 stations on 5 lines. The [Deodoro Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deodoro_Line) runs from [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil) to [Deodoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deodoro,_Rio_de_Janeiro) via [Maracanã](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracan%C3%A3,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Méier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9ier) and [Madureira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madureira,_Rio_de_Janeiro); the [Santa Cruz Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Line) runs from Central do Brasil to [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro), running parallel to the Deodoro line for its entire length and then via [Realengo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realengo), [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro); the [Japeri Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japeri_Line) shares tracks with the Santa Cruz Line between Central do Brasil and Deodoro before continuing on to [Japeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japeri) via [Anchieta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchieta,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Nilópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nil%C3%B3polis), [Mesquita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesquita,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Nova Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Igua%C3%A7u) and [Queimados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queimados); the [Belford Roxo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belford_Roxo_Line?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Saracuruna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saracuruna_Line?action=edit\&redlink=1) Lines run parallel to the Deodoro, Santa Cruz and Japeri Lines between Central do Brasil and Maracanã, with the Belford Roxo Line continuing on to [Belford Roxo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belford_Roxo) via Madureira, [Pavuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavuna) and [São João de Meriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jo%C3%A3o_de_Meriti) and the Saracuruna Line continuing on to [Duque de Caxias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duque_de_Caxias,_Rio_de_Janeiro) via [Bonsucesso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonsucesso), [Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramos,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and [Penha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penha,_Rio_de_Janeiro).[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] The suburban rail system carries around 342,000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger) per day.[^cite_note-281]

The Rio de Janeiro Light Rail is a modern [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) system that runs entirely within the [Zona Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro) on 28 km (17 mi) of track with 30 stations distributed along 4 heavily interlined lines: Line 1 runs from [Santos Dumont Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Dumont_Airport) to [Gentileza Intermodal Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentileza_Intermodal_Terminal) via [Carioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Station), the [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) and [Novo Rio Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novo_Rio_Bus_Terminal); Line 2 runs from [Praça XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%C3%A7a_XV) to [Praia Formosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praia_Formosa,_Rio_de_Janeiro?action=edit\&redlink=1) via [Praça Tiradentes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%C3%A7a_Tiradentes?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil) and Novo Rio Bus Terminal; Line 3 runs from Santos Dumont Airport to Central do Brasil via Carioca and [Pequena África](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pequena_%C3%81frica?action=edit\&redlink=1); Line 4 shares track with Line 2 for almost its entire route, but stops at Gentileza Intermodal Terminal instead of Praia Formosa.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283] The trams are the first in the world to use a combination of ground-level power supply (APS) and on-board supercapacitor energy storage (SRS), in order to eliminate overhead lines along the entire route.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-taut-2016july-285] The network uses 32 [Alstom Citadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alstom_Citadis) 402 [low-floor trams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-floor_tram) carrying 420 passengers each. They are [bi-directional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bi-directional_vehicle), [air-conditioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_conditioning), have seven sections, and eight doors per side.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-RGI-2016jun6-287] The light rail system carries 101,000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-Transbrasil/Light_Rail_passenger_numbers-288]

The Santa Teresa Tram runs from [Carioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Station) to [Santa Teresa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Teresa,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and is the oldest operating electric [tramway](30733.md) in [Latin America](18524.md),[^cite_note-morrison-TLA-289] having commenced electric operation in 1896, replacing [horse-drawn trams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horsecar) and extending the route.[^cite_note-morrison-brazil-290] At this time the gauge was altered to 1,100 mm (3 ft 7+5⁄16 in) so that the trams would fit on top of [Carioca Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca_Aqueduct).[^cite_note-morrison-brazil-290][^cite_note-291] It was designated a national historic monument in 1985.[^cite_note-morrison-brazil-290]<sup>: 111 </sup> After a [derailment](1093599.md) occurred on 27 August 2011,[^cite_note-292] which left six dead; tram service was suspended to improve the system. The elderly tramcars, which dated from the 1950s,[^cite_note-morrison-vehicles-293] were retired and replaced with newly built replicas that have the appearance of the old fleet but with new mechanical equipment and additional safety features;[^cite_note-taut-2013may-294] delivery began in 2014.[^cite_note-ODia29Sep2014-295] The line's track was also rebuilt, and after some delays, about one-third of the line reopened in July 2015. More sections reopened later in stages, following repair of additional sections of track. The main section of the line was restored to its full pre-2011 length of 6 km (4 mi) in January 2019.[^cite_note-taut-2020apr-296] The 1.7 km (1.1 mi) Paula Mattos branch to Largo das Neves, which was also closed in 2011, was reopened in January 2025.[^cite_note-297] Santa Teresa residents can ride the tram for free, but ticket prices are prohibitively expensive for other [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carioca) and, because of this, the line is primarily used by tourists.[^cite_note-298] The tram gets an average of 1900 passengers per day.[^cite_note-299]

#### Ferries



Rio de Janeiro ferry

Rio de Janeiro has [ferry](50771.md) routes running radially out from [Praça XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%C3%A7a_Quinze_de_Novembro) in the [city center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro,_Rio_de_Janeiro) to [Praça Araribóia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%C3%A7a_Ararib%C3%B3ia?action=edit\&redlink=1) (in [Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niter%C3%B3i)'s city center), [Charitas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charitas) (in the South side of Niterói), [Cocotá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocot%C3%A1) (on [Governador Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governador_Island)) and [Paquetá Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paquet%C3%A1_Island).[^cite_note-300] All four ferry routes run entirely through [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay).[^cite_note-301] The Praça XV-Praça Araribóia route is by far the busiest and carries [commuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuters) from Niterói, [São Gonçalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Gon%C3%A7alo,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Itaboraí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itabora%C3%AD) and [Maricá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maric%C3%A1).[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] Each ferry carries up to 2000 passengers,[^cite_note-304] with the ferry system as a whole carrying 35,000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-305]

#### Cable car

The [Providência Cable Car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telef%C3%A9rico_da_Provid%C3%AAncia) is a 721 m (0.45 miles) long [cable car](298681.md) line that runs from [Central do Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_do_Brasil) to [Gamboa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamboa,_Rio_de_Janeiro) via [Providência Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morro_da_Provid%C3%AAncia), serving the city's oldest [favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela).[^cite_note-306] The cable car first opened in 2014, was abandoned in 2017 and reopened in 2024.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] The line uses 16 gondolas and carries 9000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers) per day.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]

#### Inclined elevators



Pavão-Pavãozinho Inclined Elevator

Rio de Janeiro has 8 public [inclined elevators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inclined_elevator) climbing 5 [hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hills), all of which are run by the [municipal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_municipalities) through the Municipal Energy and Illumination Company (RioLuz).[^cite_note-Penna_Inclined_Elevator_Reform-311][^cite_note-312] The Santa Marta Inclined Elevator runs up [Dona Marta Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela_Santa_Marta)[^cite_note-313] in two sections with 5 total stations[^cite_note-314] and carries 5000 [passengers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passengers) per day.[^cite_note-315] The Pavão-Pavãozinho Inclined Elevator runs from [General Osório/Ipanema station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Os%C3%B3rio_Station) to [Cantagalo-Pavão-Pavãozinho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantagalo-Pav%C3%A3o-Pav%C3%A3ozinho) and consists of a single 153 m (502 ft) long line with 5 stations[^cite_note-316] that carries 4000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317] The Penna Church Inclined Elevator is 70 m (230 ft) long[^cite_note-Penna_Inclined_Elevator_Reform-311] and runs from [Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepagu%C3%A1) to Our Lady of Penna Church at the top of Penna Hill,[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] carrying 180 passengers per day.[^cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317] The Father Laércio Dias de Moura Inclined Elevator is a group of three inclined elevators[^cite_note-320] that connect [Penha station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penha_Station?action=edit\&redlink=1) to [Vila Cruzeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vila_Cruzeiro?action=edit\&redlink=1) and the [Basilica of Our Lady of Penha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Our_Lady_of_Penha?action=edit\&redlink=1);[^cite_note-321] the inclined elevators have a total length of 426 m (1,398 ft)[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] and carry 3000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317] The Outeiro da Glória Inclined Elevator is 65 m (213 ft) long and runs from [Glória station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gl%C3%B3ria_Station) to Our Lady of Glória do Outeiro Church, carrying 200 passengers per day.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-Inclined_Elevator_Passenger_Numbers_(2018)-317]

### Roads



Streetscape in Rio de Janeiro



Pedestrian infrastructure in Rio de Janeiro



[Bike Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_Rio) rental station in [Mauá Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pra%C3%A7a_Mau%C3%A1), Downtown Rio

Rio de Janeiro has 601 km (373 mi) of [limited-access roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-access_roads), 731 km (454 mi) of [arterial roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arterial_roads), 1,245 km (774 mi) of [collector roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collector_roads) and 6,678 km (4,150 mi) of [local roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street) with vehicular traffic. Despite representing 72% of roads with vehicular traffic, just 14% of [traffic collisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_collisions) and 14% of [traffic fatalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemiology_of_motor_vehicle_collisions) are on local roads; 38% of traffic fatalities happen on 20 roads, with [Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) alone accounting for 14%. Rio de Janeiro had 694 traffic deaths in 2022, equivalent to 11 deaths per 100,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-325]

Rio de Janeiro's vehicle fleet includes 3.2 million [motorized vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorized_vehicles) - equivalent to 482 per 1000 inhabitants - including 2.2 million [cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cars), 500,000 [motorcycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorcycles), 400,000 [trucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trucks) and 37,000 [buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buses) and [minibuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minibuses). 37% of vehicles use exclusively [E27](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethanol_fuel_in_Brazil) fuel; 6% use E100; 4% use [diesel fuel](81761.md), and 59% use [various combinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multifuel) of [ethanol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethanol_fuel), [gasoline](23639.md) and [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_vehicle).[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] An average of 1.6 million motorized vehicles circulate per day in the city. Approximately 47% of all trips in Rio use public transport; 23% use private motorized vehicles and 28% use [active mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_mobility).[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329]

Despite being in the [Atlantic Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Forest), Rio de Janeiro has a severe lack of [street trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_forestry). The distribution of the trees that do exist is highly unequal: poorer neighbourhoods on the North side of the city are disproportionately likely to lack tree cover, as are neighbourhoods with higher populations of [pardo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardo_Brazilians) and [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Brazilians) people. Overall, 35% of [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariocas) live on streets without any trees at all, and another 26% live on streets with fewer than 5 trees. This [environmental racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_racism) directly causes poorer neighbourhoods to suffer from more extreme weather, including more [severe flooding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_flooding) and [higher temperatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) - for example, [Lagoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagoa,_Rio_de_Janeiro) has a mean income per capita nearly 10 times higher than that of [Del Castilho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Del_Castilho) and has a mean temperature of 26 °C (79 °F), as opposed to Del Castilho's 33 °C (91 °F).[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332]

Despite Rio de Janeiro's ample [sidewalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidewalk) coverage, many sidewalks are narrow, inaccessible for [disabled people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disabled_people), and filled with obstacles. [Traffic lights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traffic_lights) frequently prioritize vehicular traffic on busy roads significantly more than pedestrian traffic, which can significantly increase pedestrian travel times. [Portuguese pavement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_pavement), which is common in Rio de Janeiro,[^cite_note-333] is extremely slippery in the rain and gets easily damaged by tree roots, which further harms pedestrian accessibility. The city has [pedestrian zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedestrian_zones) in the [city center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Paquetá Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paquet%C3%A1_Island) and concentrated around the [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervia) stations in [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu,_Rio_de_Janeiro), [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and [Penha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penha,_Rio_de_Janeiro), as well as in [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favelas). Part of the city has a wayfinding system similar to [Legible London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legible_London).[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]

Rio de Janeiro has approximately 247 km of [shared-use paths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared-use_path), 119 km of [sharrows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharrows), 64 km of segregated [cycle tracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycle_track), and 28 km of roadside [bike lanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_lanes). 16% of [cariocas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cariocas) live within 300 meters of [bicycle infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_infrastructure); the bicycle infrastructure network also reaches 30% of jobs, 16% of schools, and 21% of [health facilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_facilities). 127 of the city's 288 medium- and high-capacity public transport ([BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_Rapid_Transit), [ferry](50771.md), [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail), [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Transit) and [suburban rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban_rail)) stations are served by bicycle infrastructure. Bicycle infrastructure in the city is frequently narrow, with 66% of it failing to reach the city government's minimum standards. The city also has a [bicycle sharing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_sharing_system) called [Bike Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bike_Rio) with 430 stations and 6700 bicycles.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]

#### Major highways



[Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\))



[Rio-Niterói Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio-Niter%C3%B3i_Bridge)



[Linha Vermelha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\))

[Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) (*Brazil Avenue*) is the longest and busiest [highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-access_road) in Rio de Janeiro, stretching 58 km (36 mi) from the [Port area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) in the [city center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) through the North side to [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro) in the West side and carrying 800,000 vehicles per day.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] Almost the entire highway is part of [BR-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-101),[^cite_note-343] which continues east along the [Rio-Niterói Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio-Niter%C3%B3i_Bridge)[^cite_note-344] towards [Vitória](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vit%C3%B3ria,_Esp%C3%ADrito_Santo),[^cite_note-345] [Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador,_Bahia)[^cite_note-346] and [Recife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recife)[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348] and west through [Itaguaí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itagua%C3%AD)[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350] towards [Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo),[^cite_note-351] [Curitiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curitiba)[^cite_note-352] and [Florianópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florian%C3%B3polis).[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354] Shorter sections of Avenida Brasil are also part of [BR-040](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-040)[^cite_note-355] (which runs from the Rio de Janeiro Port area[^cite_note-356] to [Petrópolis](504174.md),[^cite_note-357] [Belo Horizonte](82198.md) and [Brasília](4752.md)),[^cite_note-358] [BR-116](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-116) (which runs northeast from Rio de Janeiro to [Teresópolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teres%C3%B3polis), [Vitória da Conquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vit%C3%B3ria_da_Conquista) and [Fortaleza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortaleza) and northwest to [São Paulo](390875.md), Curitiba and [Porto Alegre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Alegre))[^cite_note-359] and [BR-465](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-465?action=edit\&redlink=1) (which runs from the neighbourhood of [Campo Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campo_Grande,_Rio_de_Janeiro) to [Seropédica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serop%C3%A9dica)).[^cite_note-360] Avenida Brasil is a critical freight corridor and bottleneck, particularly for the Port of Rio de Janeiro, with over 500 trucks per day using the highway in the morning peak.[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] The 26 km (16 mi) long [Transbrasil BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransBrasil_\(bus_rapid_transit\)) runs in the middle of Avenida Brasil from [Gentileza Intermodal Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentileza_Intermodal_Terminal) to [Deodoro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deodoro_station) and carries 115,000 passengers per day.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-Transbrasil/Light_Rail_passenger_numbers-288]

The [Rio-Niterói Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio-Niter%C3%B3i_Bridge) is a 13 km (8 mi) long [box girder bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Box_girder_bridge) that runs between the [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) and the [Port of Niterói](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Niter%C3%B3i?action=edit\&redlink=1) via Mocanguê Island. The bridge, which is part of [BR-101](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BR-101), is the only direct road connection between the east and west sides of [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay) and is therefore a critical traffic bottleneck, carrying 150,000 vehicles per day, including over 7000 trucks. Most of the bridge is 60 m (197 ft) above the water, with the central span rising to 72 m (236 ft) to allow for large ships to pass. There is a [toll booth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toll_booth) in the eastbound direction.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

The [Linha Amarela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Line_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) (*Yellow Line*) highway, officially *Avenida Governador Carlos Lacerda*, runs 17 km (11 mi) from [Fundão Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Universit%C3%A1ria) to [Barra da Tijuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barra_da_Tijuca) via [Del Castilho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Del_Castilho), [Méier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9ier) and [Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepagu%C3%A1). It is maintained by LAMSA (Linha Amarela [S.A.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S.A._\(corporation\))), which is owned by [Invepar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invepar), and is one of two tolled roads within city limits.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] The Yellow Line carries 120,000 vehicles per day.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] It runs entirely parallel to the [Transcarioca BRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransCarioca).[^cite_note-375]

The [Linha Vermelha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Line_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) (*Red Line*), officially *Via Expressa Presidente João Goulart* or *RJ-071*, is a 22 km (14 mi) long highway that runs from [Cidade Nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Nova,_Rio_de_Janeiro) to [São João de Meriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Jo%C3%A3o_de_Meriti) via [Fundão Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Universit%C3%A1ria), [Galeão International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gale%C3%A3o_International_Airport) and [Duque de Caxias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duque_de_Caxias,_Rio_de_Janeiro), entirely parallel to [Avenida Brasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Brasil_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)).[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] There is an 8 km (5 mi) barrier known as the Wall of Shame along the highway in [Maré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar%C3%A9,_Rio_de_Janeiro); this was ostensibly built as a [noise barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noise_barrier) during the leadup to the [2016 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Summer_Olympics) but is widely considered by local residents to be a way for the city to hide the [favela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela) from Galeão International Airport passengers.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] Trucks are banned from using the highway.[^cite_note-381] The Red Line carries 90,000 vehicles per day.[^cite_note-382]

### Airports

The following airports serve the city of Rio de Janeiro:



[Rio de Janeiro/Galeão International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro/Gale%C3%A3o_International_Airport)



[Rio de Janeiro–Santos Dumont Domestic Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Dumont_Airport)

- [Galeão–Antônio Carlos Jobim International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro/Gale%C3%A3o_International_Airport): used for all international and most domestic flights. This airport is connected to local and intercity buses and the [TransCarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransCarioca) and [TransBrasil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransBrasil_\(bus_rapid_transit\)) BRTs.

  [^cite_note-383]

  [^cite_note-384]

  [^cite_note-385]

  [^cite_note-386]

- [Santos Dumont Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Dumont_Airport): used mainly for services to [São Paulo](390875.md), some short and medium-haul domestic flights, and [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation).

  [^cite_note-387]

  &#x20;As of 2025, the airport's passenger capacity is capped by the federal government at 6.5 million per year.

  [^cite_note-388]

  &#x20;The airport is connected to the city's [light rail system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Light_Rail), which connects it to several other transport modes in the city center.

  [^cite_note-389]

- [Jacarepaguá–Roberto Marinho Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepagu%C3%A1_Airport): used by general aviation and home to the Aeroclube do Brasil (Brasil [Flying club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_club)).

  [^cite_note-390]

  &#x20;It also has some short-haul commercial flights.

  [^cite_note-391]

  &#x20;The airport is located in the district of [Baixada de Jacarepaguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacarepagu%C3%A1)

  [^cite_note-392]

  &#x20;and is connected to the [TransCarioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransCarioca) and [TransOlímpica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transol%C3%ADmpica) BRTs.

  [^cite_note-393]

Air Force Bases include:

- [Galeão Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gale%C3%A3o_Air_Force_Base): A [Brazilian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Air_Force) airbase, sharing some facilities with Galeão - Antônio Carlos Jobim International Airport;

- [Santa Cruz Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Air_Force_Base): A Brazilian Air Force airbase. Formerly called [Bartolomeu de Gusmão Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolomeu_de_Gusm%C3%A3o_Airport), it was built by the [Luftschiffbau Zeppelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luftschiffbau_Zeppelin). Today it is one of the most important Air Force Bases in Brazil;

- [Afonsos Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afonsos_Air_Force_Base): One of the historical Brazilian Air Force airbases. It is also the location of the University of the Air Force (Universidade da Força Aérea),

  [^cite_note-394]

  &#x20;the [Museu Aeroespacial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Aeroespacial),

  [^cite_note-395]

  &#x20;and where air shows take place.

### Ports



[Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro)



Ilha d'Água Terminal

The [Port of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Rio_de_Janeiro) is Brazil's tenth busiest publicly-owned [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port) in terms of cargo volume,[^cite_note-ANTAQ-396] and it is the main port for [cruise vessels](314855.md). Located in the [Zona Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Central,_Rio_de_Janeiro) and on the west coast of [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay), it primarily serves the [Southeastern states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Region,_Brazil) of [Rio de Janeiro](229419.md), [São Paulo](229422.md), [Minas Gerais](222651.md), and [Espírito Santo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esp%C3%ADrito_Santo). The port is managed overall by the Companhia Docas do Rio de Janeiro, though private companies operate some parts of it. The Port of Rio de Janeiro stretches from Mauá Pier in the east[^cite_note-397] to Caju Wharf in the north and includes three [wharves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wharves) with a total length of 6,740 m (22,110 ft). Gamboa Wharf is 3,042 m (9,980 ft) long and includes 20 [berths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berth_\(moorings\)) and 19 [warehouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warehouses); this wharf is shallower than the others and therefore typically handles [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) on [Handysize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handysize) and [Handymax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handymax) ships. Since Gamboa Wharf includes the cruise ship terminal at Mauá Pier, this also prevents the [largest cruise ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cruise_ships) from docking in the port. São Cristóvão Wharf is 1,259 m (4,131 ft) long and has 12 berths and two warehouses; the eastern end of this wharf is also shallow enough to restrict shipping operations. Caju Wharf is 2,439 m (8,002 ft) long and includes 6 berths, 7 warehouses and 24 [storage tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storage_tanks), with 5,000 m<sup>3</sup> (180,000 cubic feet) of storage tank space reserved for [sodium hydroxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodium_hydroxide) and the remaining 12,000 m<sup>3</sup> (420,000 cubic feet) reserved for [petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_products). There is also a group of storage tanks just outside the port with a total capacity of 22,000 tonnes and pipelines that connect them to São Cristóvão Wharf. The berths at Caju Wharf are large enough for ships of up to 349 m (1,145 ft) [length overall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Length_overall).[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-Port_Master_Plan-399]

In 2024, the Port of Rio de Janeiro handled 15.5 million tonnes of cargo, 11.8 million tonnes of which were [containerized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerized) into 937,000 [TEUs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit). In addition to container cargo, the port also handled 1.1 million tonnes of [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron) and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel) products, 873,000 tonnes of [petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_products) (not including [crude oil](23195.md)), 444,000 tonnes of [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat), 370,000 tonnes of crude oil and 205,000 tonnes of [motor vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motor_vehicles). 10.2 million tonnes of cargo were moved internationally, 5.2 million tonnes were moved via [short-sea shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-sea_shipping), and 0.075 million tonnes were moved through [inland navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inland_navigation).[^cite_note-ANTAQ-396] The Port of Rio de Janeiro also moved 327,000 passengers on 36 [cruise ships](314855.md) making 107 calls during the 2024/25 cruise season.[^cite_note-400]

In addition to the Port of Rio de Janeiro, the city also has ten private port facilities, nine of which are on [Guanabara Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanabara_Bay) and one of which is on [Sepetiba Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepetiba_Bay?action=edit\&redlink=1). The Guanabara Bay port facilities collectively handled 16.6 million tonnes of [petroleum products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_products) in 2024 and no other cargo. 15.8 million tonnes of this cargo were shipped through the Ilha d'Água Terminal, which is owned by [Transpetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transpetro) and has a pipeline connected to [REDUC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/REDUC?action=edit\&redlink=1) ([Duque de Caxias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duque_de_Caxias,_Rio_de_Janeiro) Refinery).[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] 14 million tonnes of cargo were moved through [short-sea shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-sea_shipping) and 2.6 million tonnes were moved internationally. The port on Sepetiba Bay, which is owned by [Ternium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternium), moved 7.3 million tonnes of cargo in 2024 to support its [steel mill](1143008.md) in [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_Rio_de_Janeiro), almost all of it internationally. This port moved 3.2 million tonnes of [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron) and [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel), 2.4 million tonnes of [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal), 0.8 million tonnes of [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone), 0.4 million tonnes of [mineral oils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral_oil), 0.4 million tonnes of [iron ore](145352.md) and 0.2 million tonnes of [petroleum coke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum_coke) in 2024.[^cite_note-ANTAQ-396][^cite_note-Port_Master_Plan-399][^cite_note-403]

### Water and sewage

Further information: [Water supply and sanitation in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Brazil)

The water supply of Rio de Janeiro and the [Baixada Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baixada_Fluminense) is sourced through the interconnected Guandu, Ribeirão das Lajes and Acari [water supply systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_system). The Guandu water supply system, which uses water [diverted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbasin_transfer) from the [Paraíba do Sul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C3%ADba_do_Sul) and [Piraí Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pira%C3%AD_River_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) to the [Guandu River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guandu_River_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)), includes the largest [water treatment plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_treatment_plant) in the world, with a throughput of 43 m<sup>3</sup> (1,500 cubic feet) per second.[^cite_note-404] The Ribeirão das Lajes system uses water diverted from the Piraí River (but not the [Paraíba do Sul River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C3%ADba_do_Sul_River)) to the [Ribeirão das Lajes River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribeir%C3%A3o_das_Lajes) and supplies 5.1 m<sup>3</sup> (180 cubic feet) of water per second.[^cite_note-405] The Acari system uses five [raw water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raw_water) collection points in the [Tinguá Biological Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tingu%C3%A1_Biological_Reserve) and supplies 3 m<sup>3</sup> (110 cubic feet) of water per second.[^cite_note-406] Rio de Janeiro's [water security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_security) is threatened by its dependence on a single source, [climate change](5042951.md) and raw water pollution, particularly in the Guandu River.[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-Imunana-Laranjal-408][^cite_note-409]

[Paquetá Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paquet%C3%A1_Island), unlike the rest of Rio de Janeiro City, has its water supplied from the Imunana-Laranjal [water supply system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_system), which uses water sourced from the [Macacu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macacu_River) and [Guapiaçú Rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guapia%C3%A7%C3%BA_River) and also supplies the [Leste Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leste_Fluminense?action=edit\&redlink=1). This system treats 7 m<sup>3</sup> (250 cubic feet) of water per second.[^cite_note-410][^cite_note-Imunana-Laranjal-408]

National policy changes in 2020–2021 for the universalization of sanitation kept the 2007 policy defining the municipality as the provider of sanitation service, delegating the organization, supervision, and provision of services to third parties.[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-sanitation-412] The [National Water and Basic Sanitation Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Water_and_Basic_Sanitation_Agency?action=edit\&redlink=1) (ANA) is responsible for the setting standards regulating basic public sanitation services.[^cite_note-sanitation-412] In 2021–2022, distribution and treatment of sewage of the [Rio de Janeiro State Water and Sewage Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_Water_and_Sewage_Company?action=edit\&redlink=1) (CEDAE) was divided into four blocks and auctioned to Aegea (Águas do Rio) and Iguá; CEDAE remained in control of water collection and treatment of drinking water.[^cite_note-privatizes-water-treatment-413][^cite_note-414] The [Rio de Janeiro State Environmental Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_State_Environmental_Institute?action=edit\&redlink=1) (INEA) has primary responsibility for water pollution monitoring and enforcement.[^cite_note-degradation-415]

As of 2021 only 65% of sewage was properly treated, leaving 35% to be improperly discharged.[^cite_note-privatizes-water-treatment-413] As of 2022 there were at least 400 illicit sewage disposal points in the drainage network.[^cite_note-degradation-415]

### Solid waste

[Solid waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_waste) management in Rio de Janeiro is done by the Companhia Municipal de Limpeza Urbana (*Municipal Urban Cleaning Company* or *Comlurb*), which is the largest municipal [waste management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_management) company in [Latin America](18524.md).[^cite_note-416] Rio de Janeiro produces over 3.2 million tonnes of solid waste per year. After being collected throughout the city, solid waste is sent through one of five [waste transfer stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_transfer_station) and then on to a [sanitary landfill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitary_landfill) in [Seropédica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serop%C3%A9dica),[^cite_note-417] which produces 24,000 m³ of [biogas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogas) and 5,000 m³ of [biomethane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomethane) per hour; [leachate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leachate) is also treated and used to produce over 60m³ of [distilled water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distilled_water) per hour.[^cite_note-418] Despite 40% of the city's waste being made of recyclable materials, only 2% of it is actually [recycled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recycled).[^cite_note-419]

## Culture



[Museum of Modern Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Modern_Art,_Rio_de_Janeiro)



[National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes)



*Christ the Redeemer*

Rio de Janeiro is a main cultural hub in Brazil. Its architecture embraces churches and buildings dating from the 16th to the 19th centuries, blending with the world-renowned designs of the 20th century. Rio was home to the [Portuguese Imperial family](23033.md) and capital of the country for many years, and was influenced by [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Portugal), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_England), and [French architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_architecture).[^cite_note-420]

Rio de Janeiro has inherited a strong cultural role from the past. In the late 19th century, there were sessions held of the first Brazilian film, and since then, several production cycles have spread out, eventually placing Rio at the forefront of experimental and national cinema. The [Rio de Janeiro International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_International_Film_Festival)[^cite_note-421] has been held annually since 1999.[^cite_note-422]

Rio currently brings together the main production centers of Brazilian television.[^cite_note-423] Major international films set in Rio de Janeiro include *[Blame it on Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blame_it_on_Rio)*; the [James Bond film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bond_in_film) [*Moonraker*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonraker_\(film\)); the Oscar award-winning, critically acclaimed *[Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Station_\(film\))* by [Walter Salles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Salles), who is also one of Brazil's best-known directors; and the Oscar award-winning historical drama, *[Black Orpheus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Orpheus)*, which depicted the early days of Carnaval in Rio de Janeiro. Internationally distributed Brazilian-made movies illustrating a darker side of Rio de Janeiro include *[Elite Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Squad)* and [*City of God*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_God_\(2002_film\)).

Rio has many important cultural landmarks, such as the Biblioteca Nacional (National Library), one of the largest libraries in the world with collections totaling more than 9 million items; the [Theatro Municipal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Municipal_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)); the [National Museum of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_de_Belas_Artes); the [Carmen Miranda Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen_Miranda_Museum); the [Rio de Janeiro Botanical Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_de_Janeiro_Botanical_Garden); the [Parque Lage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parque_Lage); the [Quinta da Boa Vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinta_da_Boa_Vista); the [Imperial Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pa%C3%A7o_Imperial); the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras); the [Museu de Arte Moderna do Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Modern_Art,_Rio_de_Janeiro); and the [Natural History Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Brazil).

### Literature

Main article: [Literature of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_Brazil)



[Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras)



[National Library of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil)



[Royal Portuguese Cabinet of Reading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Portuguese_Cabinet_of_Reading)

After Brazilian independence from Portugal in 1822, Rio de Janeiro quickly developed a [European-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe) bourgeois cultural life, including numerous newspapers, in which most 19th-century novels were initially published in serial form. [Joaquim Manuel de Macedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaquim_Manuel_de_Macedo)'s *[A Moreninha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Moreninha_\(novel\))* (1844) was perhaps the first successful novel in Brazil and inaugurates a recurrent 19th-century theme: a romantic relationship between idealistic young people in spite of the cruelties of social fortune.

The first notable work of realism focusing on the urban [lower-middle class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_middle_class) is [Manuel Antônio de Almeida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Ant%C3%B4nio_de_Almeida)'s *[Memórias de um sargento de milícias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mem%C3%B3rias_de_um_sargento_de_mil%C3%ADcias)* (1854), which presents a series of picaresque scenes and evokes the transformation of a town into a city with suggestive nostalgia. Romantic and realist modes both flourished through the late 19th century and often overlapped within works.[^cite_note-424]

[Machado de Assis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machado_de_Assis) is from Rio de Janeiro, and is widely regarded as the greatest writer of [Brazilian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_literature)[^cite_note-425] and considered the founder of [Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) in Brazil, with the publication of *[The Posthumous Memoirs of Bras Cubas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Posthumous_Memoirs_of_Bras_Cubas)* (1881).[^cite_note-426] He commented on and criticized the political and social events of the city and country, such as the abolition of slavery in 1888 and the transition from [Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Brazil) to Republic, with his numerous [chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronicle) published in newspapers of the time.[^cite_note-427] Many of his short stories and novels, like *[Quincas Borba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincas_Borba)* (1891) and *[Dom Casmurro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dom_Casmurro)* (1899), are placed in Rio. The headquarters of the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras) is based in Rio de Janeiro. It was satirized by the novelist [Jorge Amado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Amado) in *[Pen, Sword, Camisole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pen,_Sword,_Camisole)*. Amado himself went on to be one of the 40 members of the academy.

The Biblioteca Nacional ([National Library of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Brazil)) ranks as one of the largest libraries in the world. It is also the largest library in all of Latin America.[^cite_note-428] Located in [Cinelândia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinel%C3%A2ndia), the National Library was created by the King of Portugal, in 1810. As with many of Rio de Janeiro's cultural monuments, the library was originally off-limits to the general public. The most valuable collections in the library include: 4,300 items donated by Barbosa Machado including a precious collection of rare brochures detailing the [History of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Portugal) and [Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Brazil); 2,365 items from the 17th and 18th centuries that were previously owned by Antônio de Araújo de Azevedo, the "Count of Barca", including the 125-volume set of prints "Le Grand Théâtre de l'Univers;" a collection of documents regarding the Jesuítica Province of Paraguay and the "Region of Prata;" and the Teresa Cristina Maria Collection, donated by [Emperor Pedro II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_II_of_Brazil). The collection contains 48,236 items. Individual items of special interest include a rare first edition of *[Os Lusíadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Os_Lus%C3%ADadas)* by [Luis de Camões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_de_Cam%C3%B5es), published in 1584; two copies of the Mogúncia Bible; and a first edition of Handel's Messiah.[^cite_note-429]

The Real Gabinete Português de Leitura (Portuguese Royal Reading Library) is located at Rua Luís de Camões, in the Centro (Downtown). The institution was founded in 1837 by a group of forty-three [Portuguese immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people), [political refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_refugee), to promote culture among the Portuguese community in the then capital of the Empire. The history of the [Brazilian Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academia_Brasileira_de_Letras) is linked to the Real Gabinete, since some of the early meetings of the academy were held there.[^cite_note-430]

### Music



[Tom Jobim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Jobim) in 1972. Considered one of the great exponents of Brazilian music, Jobim internationalized [bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova) with the help of important American artists.



[Tim Maia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Maia), the greatest representative of [soul music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music) in the country's history, from Rio de Janeiro

The official song of Rio de Janeiro is "[Cidade Maravilhosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_Maravilhosa)", which means "marvelous city". The song is considered the civic anthem of Rio, and is always the favorite song during Rio's [Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Carnival) in February. Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo are considered the center of the [urban music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_contemporary) movement in Brazil.[^cite_note-431]

"Rio was popularized by the hit song "[The Girl from Ipanema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Girl_from_Ipanema)", composed by [Antônio Carlos Jobim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B4nio_Carlos_Jobim) and [Vinicius de Moraes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinicius_de_Moraes) and recorded by [Astrud Gilberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrud_Gilberto) and [João Gilberto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Gilberto), [Frank Sinatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Sinatra), and [Ella Fitzgerald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Fitzgerald). It is also the main key song of the [bossa nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossa_nova), a [music genre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_genre) born in Rio. A genre unique to Rio and Brazil as a whole is [Funk Carioca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funk_carioca). While [samba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba) music continues to act as the national unifying agent in Rio, Funk Carioca found a strong community following in Brazil. With its genesis in the 1970s as the modern black pop music from the United States, it evolved in the 1990s to describe a variety of [electronic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_music) associated with the current US [black music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_music) scene, including [hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop), modern soul, and [house music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_music)."[^cite_note-432]

Brazil's return to democracy in 1985 allowed for a new music expression which promoted creativity and experimentation in expressive culture, in a wave of Rock'n'roll that swept the 80s. [Lobão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lob%C3%A3o_\(musician\)) emerged as the most legendary rocker in Brazil.[^cite_note-433] Commercial and cultural imports from Europe and North America have often influenced Brazil's own cultural output. For example, the hip hop that has stemmed from New York is localized into forms of musical production such as Funk Carioca and [Brazilian hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_hip_hop). Bands from Rio de Janeiro also influenced the mid-to-late development of the [Punk in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punk_in_Brazil), and that of [Brazilian thrash metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_thrash_metal). Democratic renewal also allowed for the recognition and acceptance of this diversification of Brazilian culture.[^cite_note-434]

Some of the best artists in the history of Brazilian popular music hail from Rio de Janeiro, including aforementioned Lobão, [Tim Maia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Maia), [Agepê](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agep%C3%AA), [Emílio Santiago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Em%C3%ADlio_Santiago), [Carlos Lyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Lyra), [Sandra de Sá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandra_de_S%C3%A1), [Erasmo Carlos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmo_Carlos), [Wilson Simonal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilson_Simonal), [Cazuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cazuza), [Ivan Lins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Lins), [Marcos Valle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_Valle), [Jorge Ben Jor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Ben) and [Anitta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anitta_\(singer\)).

Further information on the pop band: [R2D3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R2D3)

### Theater



[Municipal Theater of Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatro_Municipal_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\))



[City of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_das_Artes)

Rio de Janeiro's [Theatro Municipal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatro_Municipal_\(Rio_de_Janeiro\)) is one of the most attractive buildings in the central area of the city. Home of one of the largest stages in Latin America and one of Brazil's best-known venues for opera, ballet, and classical music, the building was inspired by the [Palais Garnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_Garnier), home of the [Paris Opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Opera). Construction of the Theatro Municipal began in 1905 following the designs of the architect Francisco Pereira Passos. The statues on the top, of two women representing Poetry and Music, are by [Rodolfo Bernardelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodolfo_Bernardelli), and the interior is rich with furnishings and fine paintings. Inaugurated in 1909, the Teatro Municipal has close to 1,700 seats. Its interior includes turn-of-the-century stained glass from France, ceilings of rose-colored marble, and a 1,000 pound crystal bead chandelier surrounded by a painting of the "[Dance of the Hours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_of_the_Hours)". The exterior walls of the building are dotted with inscriptions bearing the names of famous Brazilians as well as many other international celebrities.[^cite_note-435]

[Cidade das Artes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cidade_da_M%C3%BAsica) (City of Arts) is a cultural complex in Barra da Tijuca in the Southwest Zone of Rio de Janeiro, which was originally planned to open in 2004. Formally known as "Cidade da Música" (City of Music), it was finally inaugurated at the beginning of 2013. The project will host the Brazilian Symphony Orchestra, becoming a main center for music, as well as the largest modern concert hall in South America, with 1,780 seats. The complex spans approximately 90×1&#x30;~~^~~<sup>3</sup> m<sup>2</sup> (1×1&#x30;~~^~~<sup>6</sup> ft<sup>2</sup>) and also features a chamber music hall, three theaters, and 12 rehearsal rooms. From the terrace, there is a panoramic view of the zone. The building was designed by the French architect [Christian de Portzamparc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_de_Portzamparc), and construction was funded by the city of Rio de Janeiro.

A series of covered theaters collectively known as [Lona Cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lona_Cultural), administered by the city's Municipal Secretary of Culture, serve throughout the city as venues for cultural activities such as concerts, plays, workshops, art and craft fairs, and courses.

### Events



[New Year's Eve](277922.md) fireworks at Copacabana Beach

Every 31 December, 2.5 million people gather at [Copacabana Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copacabana_Beach) to celebrate New Year's in Rio de Janeiro. The crowd, mostly dressed in white, celebrates all night at the hundreds of different shows and events along the beach. It is the second-largest celebration, only next to the [Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Carnival). People celebrate the New Year by sharing chilled champagne. It is considered good luck to shake the champagne bottle and spray it around at midnight. Chilled champagne adds to the spirit of the festivities.[^cite_note-436] In January 2026, Rio de Janeiro was awarded the [Guinness world record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinness_World_Records) for the largest [New Year's Eve](277922.md) celebration by a city.**[^cite_note-437]**

[Rio Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rio_Carnival) is an annual celebration in the Roman Catholic tradition that allows merry-making and red meat consumption before the more sober 40 days of Lent penance, which culminates with Holy or Passion Week and Easter. The French or German courts probably influenced the tradition of Carnaval parades, and the custom was brought by the Portuguese or Brazilian Imperial families who had French and Austrian German ancestors. Up until the time of the [marchinhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marchinha), the revelry was more of a high-class and Caucasian-led event. The influence of the African-Brazilian drums and music became more noticeable from the first half of the 20th century. Rio de Janeiro has many Carnaval choices, including the [samba school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samba_school) (*Escolas de Samba)*[^cite_note-438] parades in the [sambadrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambadrome_Marqu%C3%AAs_de_Sapuca%C3%AD) exhibition center and the popular *[blocos de carnaval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival_block)*, street revelry, which parade in almost every corner of the city.[^cite_note-439] In 1840, the first Carnival was celebrated with a masked ball. As years passed, adorned floats and costumed revelers became a tradition among the celebrants. Carnival is known as a historic root of [Brazilian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Brazil).[^cite_note-440]

[Rock in Rio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_in_Rio) is a music festival conceived by entrepreneur Roberto Medina for the first time in 1985, and since its creation, recognized as the largest music festival in the Latin world and the largest in the world, with 1.5 million people attending the first event, 700,000 attending the second and fourth, about 1.2 million attending the third, and about 350,000 people attending each of the 3 [Lisbon](18091.md) events. It was originally organized in Rio de Janeiro, from where the name comes, and has become a world-level event and, in 2004, had its first edition abroad in Lisbon, Portugal, before [Madrid](41188263.md), Spain, and [Las Vegas](47737.md), United States. The festival is considered the eighth best in the world by the specialized site Fling Festival.[^cite_note-festivalfling-441]



Samba Parade at the *Sambódromo* (Sambadrome) during the Rio Carnival

### Sports

See also: [Sport in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Brazil)

Further information: [Football in Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Rio_de_Janeiro)



[Maracanã Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maracan%C3%A3_Stadium)



[Nilton Santos Olympic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Est%C3%A1dio_Ol%C3%ADmpico_Nilton_Santos)&#x20;



[São Januário's (Vasco da Gama) Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Janu%C3%A1rio)

As in the rest of Brazil, [association football](10568.md) is the most popular sport. The city's major teams are [Flamengo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clube_de_Regatas_do_Flamengo), [Vasco da Gama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CR_Vasco_da_Gama), [Fluminense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluminense_FC) and [Botafogo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botafogo_de_Futebol_e_Regatas). [Madureira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madureira_Esporte_Clube), [Bangu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangu_Atl%C3%A9tico_Clube), [Portuguesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associa%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Atl%C3%A9tica_Portuguesa_\(RJ\)), [America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_Football_Club_\(RJ\)) and [Bonsucesso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonsucesso_Futebol_Clube) are small clubs. Players born in the city include [Zico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zico_\(footballer\)), [Romário](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rom%C3%A1rio) and [Ronaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronaldo_\(Brazilian_footballer\)).[^cite_note-442] Rio de Janeiro was one of the host cities of the [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup) and [2014 FIFA World Cups](656933.md), for which on both occasions Brazil was the host nation.
